========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I came in because I just can't shake this thought. It's like, if all my years working as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then... then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me or lives under my roof. It's just... it's heavy, you know? 

Speaking of roofs, I actually need to get mine checked. The gutters were overflowing last time it rained, and it made this awful dripping sound on the patio. Reminded me of this time I was working a job out in the suburbs, and the homeowner had this weird fountain thing in their living room. Just water trickling down a wall. Who puts a water feature inside a house? Anyway, they wanted me to wire some track lighting around it, but I kept thinking, water and electricity, man, that's just asking for trouble. But yeah, I just... I feel like I can't trust myself anymore.

Yeah... yeah, I definitely avoid things. Like, I don't go in my own garage anymore unless I absolutely have to grab something quick, and I completely quit helping out with the electrical stuff at church. I just can't do it. And if anyone brings up car projects or, you know, the fire... I just shut them down fast. I can't talk about it. 

Speaking of the church, they actually just repainted the fellowship hall this awful shade of yellow. It's supposed to be "cheerful," I guess, but it just looks like mustard. Reminds me of this old Chevy Nova my dad used to have when I was a kid. He loved that car, spent every weekend under the hood. He used to make me hold the flashlight for him, and if I didn't hold it exactly right, he'd get so mad. "Keep it steady, boy!" he'd yell. I swear, my arm would be shaking after an hour. He ended up selling it to some guy two towns over who immediately crashed it into a telephone pole. Such a waste of a good engine. But yeah, I just... I stay away from all that stuff now. It's just too much.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Prevent something bad? No, not really. I mean, I check the locks on the doors at night, but everybody does that, right? You just want to make sure the house is secure. 

Actually, speaking of the house, I've been meaning to fix the weather stripping on the back door. It's been letting in this terrible draft lately. The other night, I swear it was howling. Sounded just like this stray dog that used to hang around my old apartment building back when I was in my twenties. We called him Buster. He was this scruffy terrier mix, always getting into the trash. One time, he dragged a whole pizza box up three flights of stairs and just left it right in front of my neighbor's door. Mrs. Higgins, I think her name was. She was furious. She always wore these bright pink curlers in her hair, even in the middle of the afternoon. She threatened to call animal control, but I think she secretly liked him. She used to leave little bowls of milk out on the fire escape. But no, I don't do anything over and over to stop bad things. Just the normal stuff.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. It's just... there's this heavy mix of guilt and anger just sitting right here in my chest, all day long. It never really goes away. Especially when I look at my nephew, you know? I look at him and I just think about how he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. It's not fair to him. 

He's a good kid, too. He just started playing Little League this spring. Plays shortstop, just like I did when I was his age. I had this coach, Coach Miller—guy used to chew tobacco during the games and spit it right into a styrofoam cup in the dugout. It was disgusting. But he knew baseball. Taught me how to turn a double play. We actually made it to the state championships that year. We lost in the bottom of the ninth, though. Jimmy Peterson dropped a pop fly right out in right field. Kid was devastated. His mom brought orange slices for everyone after the game, but nobody even wanted to eat them. We just sat there on the grass, staring at the dirt. But yeah... it's just this constant heavy feeling. I hate that my nephew has to deal with me being like this.

Reminded me of this time I was working a job out in the suburbs, and the homeowner had this weird fountain thing in their living room. Just water trickling down a wall. Who puts a water feature inside a house? Anyway, they wanted me to wire some track lighting around it, but I kept thinking, water and electricity, man, that's just asking for trouble. I look at that kid and just think about how he lost his dad and... and he got stuck with me instead. \n\nHe's a good kid, though. He likes playing baseball. We actually went to a game a couple of weeks ago, minor league, but still fun. The stadium had these really bright floodlights, and of course, I couldn't help but look at the wiring. Like, I don't go in my own garage anymore unless I absolutely have to grab something quick, and I completely quit helping out with the electrical stuff at church. I just can't do it. And if anyone brings up car projects or, you know, the fire... I just shut them down fast. I can't talk about it. \n\nSpeaking of the church, they actually just repainted the fellowship hall this awful shade of yellow. It's supposed to be \"cheerful,\" I guess, but it just looks like mustard. Reminds me of this old Chevy Nova my dad used to have when I was a kid. He loved that car, spent every weekend under the hood. He used to make me hold the flashlight for him, and if I didn't hold it exactly right, he'd get so mad. \"Keep it steady, boy!\" he'd yell. I swear, my arm would be shaking after an hour. He ended up selling it to some guy two towns over who immediately crashed it into a telephone pole. Such a waste of a good engine. But yeah, I just... It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing anything over and over to try and cope with these thoughts about your brother and your work?\n\nPatient: Over and over? I mean, I don't... I look at him and I just think about how he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. It's not fair to him. \n\nHe's a good kid, too. He just started playing Little League this spring. Plays shortstop, just like I did when I was his age. I had this coach, Coach Miller—guy used to chew tobacco during the games and spit it right into a styrofoam cup in the dugout. It was disgusting. But he knew baseball. Taught me how to turn a double play. We actually made it to the state championships that year. We lost in the bottom of the ninth, though. Jimmy Peterson dropped a pop fly right out in right field. Kid was devastated. His mom brought orange slices for everyone after the game, but nobody even wanted to eat them. We just sat there on the grass, staring at the dirt. But yeah... it's just this constant heavy feeling. I hate that my nephew has to deal with me being like this.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you feel like there was something you could have done to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / electrician / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Beliefs\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me or lives under my roof.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... yeah, I do. I mean, I've been an electrician for years. If all my years of experience couldn't keep my own brother safe, then what good is it? I came in here because I just can't shake the thought that I'm a danger to anybody who works with me now, or even anybody who lives under my roof. 

Speaking of the roof, I really need to get up there and clean out the gutters. They've been completely backed up since that massive storm we had a few weeks ago. The wind was so bad it blew half the leaves from the Johnson's oak tree right into my yard. Old man Johnson, he loves that tree. He spends hours out there just raking. He’s got this vintage riding mower he drives around on, painted bright green. Says he bought it at an auction down in Springfield back in '98. I went to an auction once, looking for some cheap tools, but I ended up buying this antique lamp instead. It had this stained glass shade with little blue dragonflies on it. My ex-wife absolutely hated it, said it was tacky, but I thought it gave off a really nice warm light in the hallway. It's just sitting in a box in the basement now, gathering dust. But... yeah. I just feel like I'm a hazard to everyone around me.

to your brother that you feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Well, no, not... not one specific thing, I guess. It's just the whole... the whole situation. I mean, we were working on that same panel together, and I just keep thinking... maybe if I had double-checked the breaker, or... I don't know.

I swear, my arm would be shaking after an hour. He ended up selling it to some guy two towns over who immediately crashed it into a telephone pole. Such a waste of a good engine. But yeah, I just... I stay away from all that stuff now. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing anything over and over to try and cope with these thoughts about your brother and your work?\n\nPatient: Over and over? I mean, I don't... I don't think so. I just try to keep my head down, you know? Just get through the day without thinking about it. \n\nI did start going to this diner down the street from my house, though. Just to get out, I guess. They make this incredible meatloaf on Thursdays. It's got this brown gravy that tastes exactly like the kind my grandma used to make. She was a character, my grandma. She had this tiny little kitchen, and she'd cook for, like, twenty people every Thanksgiving. I don't know how she fit all those pots on the stove. And she always had this old radio playing—AM station, crackling the whole time. She said the static gave the food flavor. I don't know about that, but man, she made a good pie. Cherry, usually. But yeah, I don't really do anything over and over. Just... just trying to get by.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing anything repeatedly to try and prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Prevent something bad? No, not really. I mean, I check the locks on the doors at night, but everybody does that, right? You just want to make sure the house is secure. \n\nActually, speaking of the house, I've been meaning to fix the weather stripping on the back door. It's been letting in this terrible draft lately. The other night, I swear it was howling. Sounded just like this stray dog that used to hang around my old apartment building back when I was in my twenties. We called him Buster. He was this scruffy terrier mix, always getting into the trash. One time, he dragged a whole pizza box up three flights of stairs and just left it right in front of my neighbor's door. Mrs. Higgins, I think her name was. She was furious. She always wore these bright pink curlers in her hair, even in the middle of the afternoon. She threatened to call animal control, but I think she secretly liked him. She used to leave little bowls of milk out on the fire escape. But no, I don't do anything over and over to stop bad things. Just the normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happened to your brother that you feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. It's just... there's this heavy mix of guilt and anger just sitting right here in my chest, all day long. It never really goes away. Especially when I look at my nephew, you know? I look at him and I just think about how he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. It's not fair to him. \n\nHe's a good kid, too. He just started playing Little League this spring. Plays shortstop, just like I did when I was his age. I had this coach, Coach Miller—guy used to chew tobacco during the games and spit it right into a styrofoam cup in the dugout. It was disgusting. But he knew baseball. Taught me how to turn a double play. We actually made it to the state championships that year. We lost in the bottom of the ninth, though. Jimmy Peterson dropped a pop fly right out in right field. Kid was devastated. His mom brought orange slices for everyone after the game, but nobody even wanted to eat them. We just sat there on the grass, staring at the dirt. But yeah... it's just this constant heavy feeling. I hate that my nephew has to deal with me being like this.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like there was something you could have done to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. I mean, I've been an electrician for years. If all my years of experience couldn't keep my own brother safe, then what good is it? I came in here because I just can't shake the thought that I'm a danger to anybody who works with me now, or even anybody who lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of the roof, I really need to get up there and clean out the gutters. They've been completely backed up since that massive storm we had a few weeks ago. The wind was so bad it blew half the leaves from the Johnson's oak tree right into my yard. Old man Johnson, he loves that tree. He spends hours out there just raking. He’s got this vintage riding mower he drives around on, painted bright green. Says he bought it at an auction down in Springfield back in '98. I went to an auction once, looking for some cheap tools, but I ended up buying this antique lamp instead. It had this stained glass shade with little blue dragonflies on it. My ex-wife absolutely hated it, said it was tacky, but I thought it gave off a really nice warm light in the hallway. It's just sitting in a box in the basement now, gathering dust. But... yeah. I just feel like I'm a hazard to everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think you could have done something to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I do. I just... I keep going over it in my head, thinking about what I could have checked, what I should have noticed. It's like a loop. Like when a record skips, you know? \n\nI used to collect vinyl, actually. Had a whole setup in my living room before I moved. I had this original pressing of *Dark Side of the Moon* that I found at a garage sale for like, five bucks. The guy selling it had no idea what he had. He was just trying to clear out his garage to make room for this massive boat he bought. It was one of those pontoon boats, the kind you take out on the lake and just sit on all day drinking beer. I went out on one of those once with my buddy Dave. Dave was a character—he always wore these ridiculous Hawaiian shirts, no matter what the weather was like. Even in December, he'd have a Hawaiian shirt on under his winter coat. He claimed he was trying to manifest a tropical vacation, but he never actually went anywhere. Just stayed right here in town, working at the hardware store. He could tell you exactly where every single screw and washer was in that place. It was amazing, really. But... yeah. I just can't help feeling like I should have known better. I should have seen it coming.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen during the incident with your brother that you feel you could have done differently?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not one specific thing, I guess. It's just the whole... the whole situation. I mean, we were working on that same panel together, and I just keep thinking... maybe if I had double-checked the breaker, or... I don't know. \n\nI was actually thinking about getting a new tool belt the other day. Mine is just falling apart. The leather is all cracked near the buckle. I bought it at this little hardware store down in Florida years ago when I went down there to help my uncle fix up his condo after a hurricane. He lived right on the coast, beautiful place, but man, the damage was unbelievable. We spent three weeks just ripping out drywall and pulling up soaked carpet. He had this neighbor, Mrs. Gable, who would bring us lemonade every afternoon at exactly 2:00 PM. She had these tiny little dogs, Pomeranians, I think? Just little balls of fluff that would run around yapping at everything. One of them actually tried to bite my boot once. Didn't even make a dent in the leather, obviously, but it was pretty funny watching this five-pound dog try to take down a steel-toe boot. I used to buy Red Wings, but lately I've been looking at those Thorogoods. Supposed to be better for your arches, and God knows my feet are killing me by the end of the day. But anyway... no, there wasn't like, one exact moment where I did something wrong. It's just... the whole thing just sits heavy on me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I didn't actually do anything wrong, technically speaking. There wasn't a missed step or a crossed wire that I can point to and say, "That's it, that's what I messed up." 

It's funny you mention wires, though, because I was just looking at the wiring in my own basement last night. The guy who owned the house before me, he fancied himself a bit of a handyman, but he did a terrible job. He used wire nuts that were way too small, and half the junction boxes aren't even covered. I found an old receipt of his stuffed behind one of the panels from a diner out on Route 9. The Starlight Diner, I think it was called. They used to make this incredible cherry pie. My dad would take me there sometimes on Sunday mornings when I was a kid, right after church. He always ordered his coffee black, said anything else ruined it. He had this old Chevy truck that smelled like motor oil and peppermint, because he always kept those little red and white mints in the glove compartment. I tried to find a truck like that a few years back, just for the nostalgia, you know? Looked all over Craigslist, even drove out to a farm in Ohio to check one out, but the rust was just eating right through the floorboards. The farmer had goats climbing all over it. Goats are weird animals, the way their eyes look. But... no. There wasn't anything I did or didn't do. It just... happened.

But yeah, I don't really do anything over and over. Just... just trying to get by.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing anything repeatedly to try and prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Prevent something bad? No, not really. I mean, I check the locks on the doors at night, but everybody does that, right? You just want to make sure the house is secure. \n\nActually, speaking of the house, I've been meaning to fix the weather stripping on the back door. It's been letting in this terrible draft lately. The other night, I swear it was howling. Sounded just like this stray dog that used to hang around my old apartment building back when I was in my twenties. We called him Buster. He was this scruffy terrier mix, always getting into the trash. One time, he dragged a whole pizza box up three flights of stairs and just left it right in front of my neighbor's door. Mrs. Higgins, I think her name was. She was furious. She always wore these bright pink curlers in her hair, even in the middle of the afternoon. She threatened to call animal control, but I think she secretly liked him. She used to leave little bowls of milk out on the fire escape. But no, I don't do anything over and over to stop bad things. Just the normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happened to your brother that you feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. It's just... there's this heavy mix of guilt and anger just sitting right here in my chest, all day long. It never really goes away. Especially when I look at my nephew, you know? I look at him and I just think about how he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. It's not fair to him. \n\nHe's a good kid, too. He just started playing Little League this spring. Plays shortstop, just like I did when I was his age. I had this coach, Coach Miller—guy used to chew tobacco during the games and spit it right into a styrofoam cup in the dugout. It was disgusting. But he knew baseball. Taught me how to turn a double play. We actually made it to the state championships that year. We lost in the bottom of the ninth, though. Jimmy Peterson dropped a pop fly right out in right field. Kid was devastated. His mom brought orange slices for everyone after the game, but nobody even wanted to eat them. We just sat there on the grass, staring at the dirt. But yeah... it's just this constant heavy feeling. I hate that my nephew has to deal with me being like this.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like there was something you could have done to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. I mean, I've been an electrician for years. If all my years of experience couldn't keep my own brother safe, then what good is it? I came in here because I just can't shake the thought that I'm a danger to anybody who works with me now, or even anybody who lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of the roof, I really need to get up there and clean out the gutters. They've been completely backed up since that massive storm we had a few weeks ago. The wind was so bad it blew half the leaves from the Johnson's oak tree right into my yard. Old man Johnson, he loves that tree. He spends hours out there just raking. He’s got this vintage riding mower he drives around on, painted bright green. Says he bought it at an auction down in Springfield back in '98. I went to an auction once, looking for some cheap tools, but I ended up buying this antique lamp instead. It had this stained glass shade with little blue dragonflies on it. My ex-wife absolutely hated it, said it was tacky, but I thought it gave off a really nice warm light in the hallway. It's just sitting in a box in the basement now, gathering dust. But... yeah. I just feel like I'm a hazard to everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think you could have done something to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I do. I just... I keep going over it in my head, thinking about what I could have checked, what I should have noticed. It's like a loop. Like when a record skips, you know? \n\nI used to collect vinyl, actually. Had a whole setup in my living room before I moved. I had this original pressing of *Dark Side of the Moon* that I found at a garage sale for like, five bucks. The guy selling it had no idea what he had. He was just trying to clear out his garage to make room for this massive boat he bought. It was one of those pontoon boats, the kind you take out on the lake and just sit on all day drinking beer. I went out on one of those once with my buddy Dave. Dave was a character—he always wore these ridiculous Hawaiian shirts, no matter what the weather was like. Even in December, he'd have a Hawaiian shirt on under his winter coat. He claimed he was trying to manifest a tropical vacation, but he never actually went anywhere. Just stayed right here in town, working at the hardware store. He could tell you exactly where every single screw and washer was in that place. It was amazing, really. But... yeah. I just can't help feeling like I should have known better. I should have seen it coming.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen during the incident with your brother that you feel you could have done differently?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not one specific thing, I guess. It's just the whole... the whole situation. I mean, we were working on that same panel together, and I just keep thinking... maybe if I had double-checked the breaker, or... I don't know. \n\nI was actually thinking about getting a new tool belt the other day. Mine is just falling apart. The leather is all cracked near the buckle. I bought it at this little hardware store down in Florida years ago when I went down there to help my uncle fix up his condo after a hurricane. He lived right on the coast, beautiful place, but man, the damage was unbelievable. We spent three weeks just ripping out drywall and pulling up soaked carpet. He had this neighbor, Mrs. Gable, who would bring us lemonade every afternoon at exactly 2:00 PM. She had these tiny little dogs, Pomeranians, I think? Just little balls of fluff that would run around yapping at everything. One of them actually tried to bite my boot once. Didn't even make a dent in the leather, obviously, but it was pretty funny watching this five-pound dog try to take down a steel-toe boot. I used to buy Red Wings, but lately I've been looking at those Thorogoods. Supposed to be better for your arches, and God knows my feet are killing me by the end of the day. But anyway... no, there wasn't like, one exact moment where I did something wrong. It's just... the whole thing just sits heavy on me.\n\nDoctor: Did you do something or fail to do something during the incident with your brother that you now wish you had handled differently?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I didn't actually do anything wrong, technically speaking. There wasn't a missed step or a crossed wire that I can point to and say, \"That's it, that's what I messed up.\" \n\nIt's funny you mention wires, though, because I was just looking at the wiring in my own basement last night. The guy who owned the house before me, he fancied himself a bit of a handyman, but he did a terrible job. He used wire nuts that were way too small, and half the junction boxes aren't even covered. I found an old receipt of his stuffed behind one of the panels from a diner out on Route 9. The Starlight Diner, I think it was called. They used to make this incredible cherry pie. My dad would take me there sometimes on Sunday mornings when I was a kid, right after church. He always ordered his coffee black, said anything else ruined it. He had this old Chevy truck that smelled like motor oil and peppermint, because he always kept those little red and white mints in the glove compartment. I tried to find a truck like that a few years back, just for the nostalgia, you know? Looked all over Craigslist, even drove out to a farm in Ohio to check one out, but the rust was just eating right through the floorboards. The farmer had goats climbing all over it. Goats are weird animals, the way their eyes look. But... no. There wasn't anything I did or didn't do. It just... happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean... that's exactly it. That's the whole reason I came in here to talk to you. I just can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then... then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me. Or even anyone who just lives under my roof. 

Speaking of roofs, I actually had to get mine patched up last spring. We had this crazy hail storm that just came out of nowhere. Golf ball-sized hail, just completely destroying the shingles. The guy who came out to do the estimate, he drove this old El Camino. You really don't see those around much anymore, you know? It was painted this bright, cherry red. Reminded me of my cousin Tommy's car back in high school. Tommy was obsessed with restoring classic cars, but he was honestly terrible at it. He'd take the engine apart and then just leave all the parts scattered all over his mom's driveway for months. She'd get so mad because she couldn't park her minivan. She drove one of those early 90s Dodge Caravans with the fake wood paneling on the side. We took that thing on a road trip to Mount Rushmore once when we were kids. The AC broke halfway through South Dakota, and it was like ninety-five degrees out. We had to roll all the windows down, and the wind was just howling so loud you couldn't even hear the radio. My aunt kept trying to play these John Denver cassettes... 

But, I mean, yeah. If my own experience wasn't enough to protect him, I just feel like I'm a walking hazard to everyone else.

Reminded me of this time I was working a job out in the suburbs, and the homeowner had this weird fountain thing in their living room. Just water trickling down a wall. Who puts a water feature inside a house? Anyway, they wanted me to wire some track lighting around it, but I kept thinking, water and electricity, man, that's just asking for trouble. I look at that kid and just think about how he lost his dad and... and he got stuck with me instead. \n\nHe's a good kid, though. He likes playing baseball. Just to get out, I guess. They make this incredible meatloaf on Thursdays. It's got this brown gravy that tastes exactly like the kind my grandma used to make. She was a character, my grandma. She had this tiny little kitchen, and she'd cook for, like, twenty people every Thanksgiving. I don't know how she fit all those pots on the stove. And she always had this old radio playing—AM station, crackling the whole time. She said the static gave the food flavor. I don't know about that, but man, she made a good pie. Cherry, usually. But yeah, I don't really do anything over and over. Just... just trying to get by.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing anything repeatedly to try and prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Prevent something bad? No, not really. The other night, I swear it was howling. Sounded just like this stray dog that used to hang around my old apartment building back when I was in my twenties. We called him Buster. He was this scruffy terrier mix, always getting into the trash. One time, he dragged a whole pizza box up three flights of stairs and just left it right in front of my neighbor's door. Mrs. Higgins, I think her name was. She was furious. She always wore these bright pink curlers in her hair, even in the middle of the afternoon. She threatened to call animal control, but I think she secretly liked him. She used to leave little bowls of milk out on the fire escape. But no, I don't do anything over and over to stop bad things. Just the normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happened to your brother that you feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. It's just... there's this heavy mix of guilt and anger just sitting right here in my chest, all day long. It never really goes away. Especially when I look at my nephew, you know? I look at him and I just think about how he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. It's not fair to him. \n\nHe's a good kid, too. He just started playing Little League this spring. Plays shortstop, just like I did when I was his age. I had this coach, Coach Miller—guy used to chew tobacco during the games and spit it right into a styrofoam cup in the dugout. It was disgusting. But he knew baseball. Taught me how to turn a double play. We actually made it to the state championships that year. We lost in the bottom of the ninth, though. Jimmy Peterson dropped a pop fly right out in right field. Kid was devastated. His mom brought orange slices for everyone after the game, but nobody even wanted to eat them. We just sat there on the grass, staring at the dirt. But yeah... it's just this constant heavy feeling. Old man Johnson, he loves that tree. He spends hours out there just raking. He’s got this vintage riding mower he drives around on, painted bright green. Says he bought it at an auction down in Springfield back in '98. I went to an auction once, looking for some cheap tools, but I ended up buying this antique lamp instead. It had this stained glass shade with little blue dragonflies on it. My ex-wife absolutely hated it, said it was tacky, but I thought it gave off a really nice warm light in the hallway. It's just sitting in a box in the basement now, gathering dust. But... yeah. I just feel like I'm a hazard to everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think you could have done something to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... It had this stained glass shade with little blue dragonflies on it. My ex-wife absolutely hated it, said it was tacky, but I thought it gave off a really nice warm light in the hallway. It's just sitting in a box in the basement now, gathering dust. But... yeah. I do. I just... I keep going over it in my head, thinking about what I could have checked, what I should have noticed. It's like a loop. I had this original pressing of *Dark Side of the Moon* that I found at a garage sale for like, five bucks. The guy selling it had no idea what he had. He was just trying to clear out his garage to make room for this massive boat he bought. It was one of those pontoon boats, the kind you take out on the lake and just sit on all day drinking beer. I went out on one of those once with my buddy Dave. Dave was a character—he always wore these ridiculous Hawaiian shirts, no matter what the weather was like. Even in December, he'd have a Hawaiian shirt on under his winter coat. He claimed he was trying to manifest a tropical vacation, but he never actually went anywhere. Just stayed right here in town, working at the hardware store. He could tell you exactly where every single screw and washer was in that place. It was amazing, really. But... yeah. I just can't help feeling like I should have known better. I don't know. \n\nI was actually thinking about getting a new tool belt the other day. Mine is just falling apart. The leather is all cracked near the buckle. I bought it at this little hardware store down in Florida years ago when I went down there to help my uncle fix up his condo after a hurricane. He lived right on the coast, beautiful place, but man, the damage was unbelievable. We spent three weeks just ripping out drywall and pulling up soaked carpet. He had this neighbor, Mrs. Gable, who would bring us lemonade every afternoon at exactly 2:00 PM. She had these tiny little dogs, Pomeranians, I think? Just little balls of fluff that would run around yapping at everything. One of them actually tried to bite my boot once. Didn't even make a dent in the leather, obviously, but it was pretty funny watching this five-pound dog try to take down a steel-toe boot. I used to buy Red Wings, but lately I've been looking at those Thorogoods. Supposed to be better for your arches, and God knows my feet are killing me by the end of the day. No, there wasn't... I mean, I've racked my brain a million times, and I can't think of one single thing I actually did wrong. Or forgot to do. It's just... the whole thing. 

I was actually just looking at the tools I had with me that day. They're still sitting in the garage in this old metal toolbox my grandfather gave me. He was a machinist down in Philly for, like, forty years. Tough guy. He had this crazy scar on his forearm from where a piece of sheet metal caught him. He used to tell me he got it fighting a bear in the Poconos. I believed him until I was about ten. We used to go up to the Poconos every summer, actually, to this cabin near Lake Wallenpaupack. The mosquitoes up there were just massive. We used to burn those citronella candles, the ones in the little tin buckets, but they never really worked. My cousin got bit so bad once his eye swelled completely shut. He looked like he'd gone a few rounds with Mike Tyson. Tyson had that fight... when was it, '97? The one where he bit Holyfield's ear? Man, I remember watching that on pay-per-view at my buddy Dave's house. Dave made these incredible nachos with, like, three different kinds of cheese and jalapeños he grew in his own backyard. He had a real green thumb, Dave did. Grew tomatoes the size of softballs. 

But no, honestly, there wasn't any specific mistake I made. I just... I don't know.

Just to get out, I guess. They make this incredible meatloaf on Thursdays. It's got this brown gravy that tastes exactly like the kind my grandma used to make. She was a character, my grandma. She had this tiny little kitchen, and she'd cook for, like, twenty people every Thanksgiving. I don't know how she fit all those pots on the stove. And she always had this old radio playing—AM station, crackling the whole time. She said the static gave the food flavor. I don't know about that, but man, she made a good pie. Cherry, usually. But yeah, I don't really do anything over and over. Just... just trying to get by.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing anything repeatedly to try and prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Prevent something bad? No, not really. I mean, I check the locks on the doors at night, but everybody does that, right? You just want to make sure the house is secure. \n\nActually, speaking of the house, I've been meaning to fix the weather stripping on the back door. It's been letting in this terrible draft lately. The other night, I swear it was howling. Sounded just like this stray dog that used to hang around my old apartment building back when I was in my twenties. We called him Buster. He was this scruffy terrier mix, always getting into the trash. One time, he dragged a whole pizza box up three flights of stairs and just left it right in front of my neighbor's door. Mrs. Higgins, I think her name was. She was furious. She always wore these bright pink curlers in her hair, even in the middle of the afternoon. She threatened to call animal control, but I think she secretly liked him. She used to leave little bowls of milk out on the fire escape. But no, I don't do anything over and over to stop bad things. Just the normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happened to your brother that you feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. It's just... there's this heavy mix of guilt and anger just sitting right here in my chest, all day long. It never really goes away. Especially when I look at my nephew, you know? I look at him and I just think about how he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. It's not fair to him. \n\nHe's a good kid, too. He just started playing Little League this spring. Plays shortstop, just like I did when I was his age. I had this coach, Coach Miller—guy used to chew tobacco during the games and spit it right into a styrofoam cup in the dugout. It was disgusting. But he knew baseball. Taught me how to turn a double play. We actually made it to the state championships that year. We lost in the bottom of the ninth, though. Jimmy Peterson dropped a pop fly right out in right field. Kid was devastated. His mom brought orange slices for everyone after the game, but nobody even wanted to eat them. We just sat there on the grass, staring at the dirt. But yeah... it's just this constant heavy feeling. I hate that my nephew has to deal with me being like this.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like there was something you could have done to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. I mean, I've been an electrician for years. If all my years of experience couldn't keep my own brother safe, then what good is it? I came in here because I just can't shake the thought that I'm a danger to anybody who works with me now, or even anybody who lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of the roof, I really need to get up there and clean out the gutters. They've been completely backed up since that massive storm we had a few weeks ago. The wind was so bad it blew half the leaves from the Johnson's oak tree right into my yard. Old man Johnson, he loves that tree. He spends hours out there just raking. He’s got this vintage riding mower he drives around on, painted bright green. Says he bought it at an auction down in Springfield back in '98. I went to an auction once, looking for some cheap tools, but I ended up buying this antique lamp instead. It had this stained glass shade with little blue dragonflies on it. My ex-wife absolutely hated it, said it was tacky, but I thought it gave off a really nice warm light in the hallway. It's just sitting in a box in the basement now, gathering dust. But... yeah. I just feel like I'm a hazard to everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think you could have done something to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I do. I just... I keep going over it in my head, thinking about what I could have checked, what I should have noticed. It's like a loop. Like when a record skips, you know? \n\nI used to collect vinyl, actually. Had a whole setup in my living room before I moved. I had this original pressing of *Dark Side of the Moon* that I found at a garage sale for like, five bucks. The guy selling it had no idea what he had. He was just trying to clear out his garage to make room for this massive boat he bought. It was one of those pontoon boats, the kind you take out on the lake and just sit on all day drinking beer. I went out on one of those once with my buddy Dave. Dave was a character—he always wore these ridiculous Hawaiian shirts, no matter what the weather was like. Even in December, he'd have a Hawaiian shirt on under his winter coat. He claimed he was trying to manifest a tropical vacation, but he never actually went anywhere. Just stayed right here in town, working at the hardware store. He could tell you exactly where every single screw and washer was in that place. It was amazing, really. But... yeah. I just can't help feeling like I should have known better. I should have seen it coming.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen during the incident with your brother that you feel you could have done differently?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not one specific thing, I guess. It's just the whole... the whole situation. I mean, we were working on that same panel together, and I just keep thinking... maybe if I had double-checked the breaker, or... I don't know. \n\nI was actually thinking about getting a new tool belt the other day. Mine is just falling apart. The leather is all cracked near the buckle. I bought it at this little hardware store down in Florida years ago when I went down there to help my uncle fix up his condo after a hurricane. He lived right on the coast, beautiful place, but man, the damage was unbelievable. We spent three weeks just ripping out drywall and pulling up soaked carpet. He had this neighbor, Mrs. Gable, who would bring us lemonade every afternoon at exactly 2:00 PM. She had these tiny little dogs, Pomeranians, I think? Just little balls of fluff that would run around yapping at everything. One of them actually tried to bite my boot once. Didn't even make a dent in the leather, obviously, but it was pretty funny watching this five-pound dog try to take down a steel-toe boot. I used to buy Red Wings, but lately I've been looking at those Thorogoods. Supposed to be better for your arches, and God knows my feet are killing me by the end of the day. But anyway... no, there wasn't like, one exact moment where I did something wrong. It's just... the whole thing just sits heavy on me.\n\nDoctor: Did you do something or fail to do something during the incident with your brother that you now wish you had handled differently?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I didn't actually do anything wrong, technically speaking. There wasn't a missed step or a crossed wire that I can point to and say, \"That's it, that's what I messed up.\" \n\nIt's funny you mention wires, though, because I was just looking at the wiring in my own basement last night. The guy who owned the house before me, he fancied himself a bit of a handyman, but he did a terrible job. He used wire nuts that were way too small, and half the junction boxes aren't even covered. I found an old receipt of his stuffed behind one of the panels from a diner out on Route 9. The Starlight Diner, I think it was called. They used to make this incredible cherry pie. My dad would take me there sometimes on Sunday mornings when I was a kid, right after church. He always ordered his coffee black, said anything else ruined it. He had this old Chevy truck that smelled like motor oil and peppermint, because he always kept those little red and white mints in the glove compartment. I tried to find a truck like that a few years back, just for the nostalgia, you know? Looked all over Craigslist, even drove out to a farm in Ohio to check one out, but the rust was just eating right through the floorboards. The farmer had goats climbing all over it. Goats are weird animals, the way their eyes look. But... no. There wasn't anything I did or didn't do. It just... happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your brother's death would have been prevented if you had acted differently in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... that's exactly it. That's the whole reason I came in here to talk to you. I just can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then... then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me. Or even anyone who just lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of roofs, I actually had to get mine patched up last spring. We had this crazy hail storm that just came out of nowhere. Golf ball-sized hail, just completely destroying the shingles. The guy who came out to do the estimate, he drove this old El Camino. You really don't see those around much anymore, you know? It was painted this bright, cherry red. Reminded me of my cousin Tommy's car back in high school. Tommy was obsessed with restoring classic cars, but he was honestly terrible at it. He'd take the engine apart and then just leave all the parts scattered all over his mom's driveway for months. She'd get so mad because she couldn't park her minivan. She drove one of those early 90s Dodge Caravans with the fake wood paneling on the side. We took that thing on a road trip to Mount Rushmore once when we were kids. The AC broke halfway through South Dakota, and it was like ninety-five degrees out. We had to roll all the windows down, and the wind was just howling so loud you couldn't even hear the radio. My aunt kept trying to play these John Denver cassettes... \n\nBut, I mean, yeah. If my own experience wasn't enough to protect him, I just feel like I'm a walking hazard to everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific you did or didn't do during the incident with your brother that you think might have contributed to the outcome?\n\nPatient: No, there wasn't... I mean, I've racked my brain a million times, and I can't think of one single thing I actually did wrong. Or forgot to do. It's just... the whole thing. \n\nI was actually just looking at the tools I had with me that day. They're still sitting in the garage in this old metal toolbox my grandfather gave me. He was a machinist down in Philly for, like, forty years. Tough guy. He had this crazy scar on his forearm from where a piece of sheet metal caught him. He used to tell me he got it fighting a bear in the Poconos. I believed him until I was about ten. We used to go up to the Poconos every summer, actually, to this cabin near Lake Wallenpaupack. The mosquitoes up there were just massive. We used to burn those citronella candles, the ones in the little tin buckets, but they never really worked. My cousin got bit so bad once his eye swelled completely shut. He looked like he'd gone a few rounds with Mike Tyson. Tyson had that fight... when was it, '97? The one where he bit Holyfield's ear? Man, I remember watching that on pay-per-view at my buddy Dave's house. Dave made these incredible nachos with, like, three different kinds of cheese and jalapeños he grew in his own backyard. He had a real green thumb, Dave did. Grew tomatoes the size of softballs. \n\nBut no, honestly, there wasn't any specific mistake I made. I just... I don't know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, I mean... it's not even about a specific mistake, like I said. It's just this... this heavy mix of guilt and anger that just sits right here in my chest all day long. Especially when I look at my nephew. I look at him and I just think... he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead.

I used to use shaving cream to break in my gloves when I was in Little League. Shaving cream and then you'd wrap it in rubber bands and stick it under your mattress for a week. My mom hated it because it made the whole room smell like Barbasol. She always bought the cheap brand, never the gel stuff. She was super frugal like that, you know? Like, she would save those little plastic bread ties and use them for everything. Organizing cords, tying up tomato plants... She had this huge garden out back. Grew the biggest zucchini I've ever seen. We had to eat zucchini bread, zucchini boats, fried zucchini, all summer long. I can't even look at a zucchini now without feeling slightly nauseous. But anyway, yeah, the guilt is just... it's just always there, weighing me down.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, it's like I was just saying a minute ago... it really isn't about some specific move I should have made differently, or a wire I should've checked twice. It's just this... this constant, heavy mix of guilt and anger. It just sits right here in my chest all day long. I actually had to take him to his dentist appointment on Tuesday. Dr. Henderson over on Elm Street. His office has this huge fish tank in the waiting room. Saltwater tank. Got these little clownfish in it, some tangs. I always wanted a saltwater tank, but I heard they're a total nightmare to keep clean. The pH balance gets messed up and everything just dies overnight. My buddy Greg had one, and he spent like two grand on live coral alone, just for his water heater to break while he was on vacation in Florida. He went down to Key West for a bachelor party. It's supposed to be \"cheerful,\" I guess, but it just looks like mustard. Reminds me of this old Chevy Nova my dad used to have when I was a kid. He loved that car, spent every weekend under the hood. He used to make me hold the flashlight for him, and if I didn't hold it exactly right, he'd get so mad. \"Keep it steady, boy!\" he'd yell. I swear, my arm would be shaking after an hour. He ended up selling it to some guy two towns over who immediately crashed it into a telephone pole. Such a waste of a good engine. But yeah, I just... I stay away from all that stuff now. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing anything over and over to try and cope with these thoughts about your brother and your work?\n\nPatient: Over and over? I mean, I don't... I don't think so. I just try to keep my head down, you know? Just get through the day without thinking about it. \n\nI did start going to this diner down the street from my house, though. Just to get out, I guess. They make this incredible meatloaf on Thursdays. It's got this brown gravy that tastes exactly like the kind my grandma used to make. She was a character, my grandma. She had this tiny little kitchen, and she'd cook for, like, twenty people every Thanksgiving. I don't know how she fit all those pots on the stove. And she always had this old radio playing—AM station, crackling the whole time. She said the static gave the food flavor. I don't know about that, but man, she made a good pie. Cherry, usually. But yeah, I don't really do anything over and over. Just... just trying to get by.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing anything repeatedly to try and prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Prevent something bad? No, not really. I mean, I check the locks on the doors at night, but everybody does that, right? You just want to make sure the house is secure. \n\nActually, speaking of the house, I've been meaning to fix the weather stripping on the back door. It's been letting in this terrible draft lately. The other night, I swear it was howling. Sounded just like this stray dog that used to hang around my old apartment building back when I was in my twenties. We called him Buster. He was this scruffy terrier mix, always getting into the trash. One time, he dragged a whole pizza box up three flights of stairs and just left it right in front of my neighbor's door. Mrs. Higgins, I think her name was. She was furious. She always wore these bright pink curlers in her hair, even in the middle of the afternoon. She threatened to call animal control, but I think she secretly liked him. She used to leave little bowls of milk out on the fire escape. But no, I don't do anything over and over to stop bad things. Just the normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happened to your brother that you feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. It's just... there's this heavy mix of guilt and anger just sitting right here in my chest, all day long. It never really goes away. Especially when I look at my nephew, you know? I look at him and I just think about how he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. It's not fair to him. \n\nHe's a good kid, too. He just started playing Little League this spring. Plays shortstop, just like I did when I was his age. I had this coach, Coach Miller—guy used to chew tobacco during the games and spit it right into a styrofoam cup in the dugout. It was disgusting. But he knew baseball. Taught me how to turn a double play. We actually made it to the state championships that year. We lost in the bottom of the ninth, though. Jimmy Peterson dropped a pop fly right out in right field. Kid was devastated. His mom brought orange slices for everyone after the game, but nobody even wanted to eat them. We just sat there on the grass, staring at the dirt. But yeah... it's just this constant heavy feeling. I hate that my nephew has to deal with me being like this.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like there was something you could have done to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. I mean, I've been an electrician for years. If all my years of experience couldn't keep my own brother safe, then what good is it? I came in here because I just can't shake the thought that I'm a danger to anybody who works with me now, or even anybody who lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of the roof, I really need to get up there and clean out the gutters. They've been completely backed up since that massive storm we had a few weeks ago. The wind was so bad it blew half the leaves from the Johnson's oak tree right into my yard. Old man Johnson, he loves that tree. He spends hours out there just raking. He’s got this vintage riding mower he drives around on, painted bright green. Says he bought it at an auction down in Springfield back in '98. I went to an auction once, looking for some cheap tools, but I ended up buying this antique lamp instead. It had this stained glass shade with little blue dragonflies on it. My ex-wife absolutely hated it, said it was tacky, but I thought it gave off a really nice warm light in the hallway. It's just sitting in a box in the basement now, gathering dust. But... yeah. I just feel like I'm a hazard to everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think you could have done something to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I do. I just... I keep going over it in my head, thinking about what I could have checked, what I should have noticed. It's like a loop. Like when a record skips, you know? \n\nI used to collect vinyl, actually. Had a whole setup in my living room before I moved. I had this original pressing of *Dark Side of the Moon* that I found at a garage sale for like, five bucks. The guy selling it had no idea what he had. He was just trying to clear out his garage to make room for this massive boat he bought. It was one of those pontoon boats, the kind you take out on the lake and just sit on all day drinking beer. I went out on one of those once with my buddy Dave. Dave was a character—he always wore these ridiculous Hawaiian shirts, no matter what the weather was like. Even in December, he'd have a Hawaiian shirt on under his winter coat. He claimed he was trying to manifest a tropical vacation, but he never actually went anywhere. Just stayed right here in town, working at the hardware store. He could tell you exactly where every single screw and washer was in that place. It was amazing, really. But... yeah. I just can't help feeling like I should have known better. I should have seen it coming.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen during the incident with your brother that you feel you could have done differently?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not one specific thing, I guess. It's just the whole... the whole situation. I mean, we were working on that same panel together, and I just keep thinking... maybe if I had double-checked the breaker, or... I don't know. \n\nI was actually thinking about getting a new tool belt the other day. Mine is just falling apart. The leather is all cracked near the buckle. I bought it at this little hardware store down in Florida years ago when I went down there to help my uncle fix up his condo after a hurricane. He lived right on the coast, beautiful place, but man, the damage was unbelievable. We spent three weeks just ripping out drywall and pulling up soaked carpet. He had this neighbor, Mrs. Gable, who would bring us lemonade every afternoon at exactly 2:00 PM. She had these tiny little dogs, Pomeranians, I think? Just little balls of fluff that would run around yapping at everything. One of them actually tried to bite my boot once. Didn't even make a dent in the leather, obviously, but it was pretty funny watching this five-pound dog try to take down a steel-toe boot. I used to buy Red Wings, but lately I've been looking at those Thorogoods. Supposed to be better for your arches, and God knows my feet are killing me by the end of the day. But anyway... no, there wasn't like, one exact moment where I did something wrong. It's just... the whole thing just sits heavy on me.\n\nDoctor: Did you do something or fail to do something during the incident with your brother that you now wish you had handled differently?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I didn't actually do anything wrong, technically speaking. There wasn't a missed step or a crossed wire that I can point to and say, \"That's it, that's what I messed up.\" \n\nIt's funny you mention wires, though, because I was just looking at the wiring in my own basement last night. The guy who owned the house before me, he fancied himself a bit of a handyman, but he did a terrible job. He used wire nuts that were way too small, and half the junction boxes aren't even covered. I found an old receipt of his stuffed behind one of the panels from a diner out on Route 9. The Starlight Diner, I think it was called. They used to make this incredible cherry pie. My dad would take me there sometimes on Sunday mornings when I was a kid, right after church. He always ordered his coffee black, said anything else ruined it. He had this old Chevy truck that smelled like motor oil and peppermint, because he always kept those little red and white mints in the glove compartment. I tried to find a truck like that a few years back, just for the nostalgia, you know? Looked all over Craigslist, even drove out to a farm in Ohio to check one out, but the rust was just eating right through the floorboards. The farmer had goats climbing all over it. Goats are weird animals, the way their eyes look. But... no. There wasn't anything I did or didn't do. It just... happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your brother's death would have been prevented if you had acted differently in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... that's exactly it. That's the whole reason I came in here to talk to you. I just can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then... then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me. Or even anyone who just lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of roofs, I actually had to get mine patched up last spring. We had this crazy hail storm that just came out of nowhere. Golf ball-sized hail, just completely destroying the shingles. The guy who came out to do the estimate, he drove this old El Camino. You really don't see those around much anymore, you know? It was painted this bright, cherry red. Reminded me of my cousin Tommy's car back in high school. Tommy was obsessed with restoring classic cars, but he was honestly terrible at it. He'd take the engine apart and then just leave all the parts scattered all over his mom's driveway for months. She'd get so mad because she couldn't park her minivan. She drove one of those early 90s Dodge Caravans with the fake wood paneling on the side. We took that thing on a road trip to Mount Rushmore once when we were kids. The AC broke halfway through South Dakota, and it was like ninety-five degrees out. We had to roll all the windows down, and the wind was just howling so loud you couldn't even hear the radio. My aunt kept trying to play these John Denver cassettes... \n\nBut, I mean, yeah. If my own experience wasn't enough to protect him, I just feel like I'm a walking hazard to everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific you did or didn't do during the incident with your brother that you think might have contributed to the outcome?\n\nPatient: No, there wasn't... I mean, I've racked my brain a million times, and I can't think of one single thing I actually did wrong. Or forgot to do. It's just... the whole thing. \n\nI was actually just looking at the tools I had with me that day. They're still sitting in the garage in this old metal toolbox my grandfather gave me. He was a machinist down in Philly for, like, forty years. Tough guy. He had this crazy scar on his forearm from where a piece of sheet metal caught him. He used to tell me he got it fighting a bear in the Poconos. I believed him until I was about ten. We used to go up to the Poconos every summer, actually, to this cabin near Lake Wallenpaupack. The mosquitoes up there were just massive. We used to burn those citronella candles, the ones in the little tin buckets, but they never really worked. My cousin got bit so bad once his eye swelled completely shut. He looked like he'd gone a few rounds with Mike Tyson. Tyson had that fight... when was it, '97? The one where he bit Holyfield's ear? Man, I remember watching that on pay-per-view at my buddy Dave's house. Dave made these incredible nachos with, like, three different kinds of cheese and jalapeños he grew in his own backyard. He had a real green thumb, Dave did. Grew tomatoes the size of softballs. \n\nBut no, honestly, there wasn't any specific mistake I made. I just... I don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because of something you did or didn't do during the incident with your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... it's not even about a specific mistake, like I said. It's just this... this heavy mix of guilt and anger that just sits right here in my chest all day long. Especially when I look at my nephew. I look at him and I just think... he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. \n\nWe were actually trying to play catch in the yard the other day. I bought him this new baseball glove, a Rawlings. Real nice leather, took forever to break in. I used to use shaving cream to break in my gloves when I was in Little League. Shaving cream and then you'd wrap it in rubber bands and stick it under your mattress for a week. My mom hated it because it made the whole room smell like Barbasol. She always bought the cheap brand, never the gel stuff. She was super frugal like that, you know? Like, she would save those little plastic bread ties and use them for everything. Organizing cords, tying up tomato plants... She had this huge garden out back. Grew the biggest zucchini I've ever seen. We had to eat zucchini bread, zucchini boats, fried zucchini, all summer long. I can't even look at a zucchini now without feeling slightly nauseous. But anyway, yeah, the guilt is just... it's just always there, weighing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think there was something you should have done differently when you were working with your brother?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's like I was just saying a minute ago... it really isn't about some specific move I should have made differently, or a wire I should've checked twice. It's just this... this constant, heavy mix of guilt and anger. It just sits right here in my chest all day long. And it gets so much worse when I look at my nephew, knowing he lost his dad and now he's just... stuck with me instead. \n\nI actually had to take him to his dentist appointment on Tuesday. Dr. Henderson over on Elm Street. His office has this huge fish tank in the waiting room. Saltwater tank. Got these little clownfish in it, some tangs. I always wanted a saltwater tank, but I heard they're a total nightmare to keep clean. The pH balance gets messed up and everything just dies overnight. My buddy Greg had one, and he spent like two grand on live coral alone, just for his water heater to break while he was on vacation in Florida. He went down to Key West for a bachelor party. They rented this pontoon boat and ended up getting stranded on a sandbar for like six hours. Said they ran out of beer in the first hour and just had to sit there baking in the sun until the tide came back in. He got a sunburn so bad his shoulders were peeling for a month... \n\nBut anyway. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Like I've been saying, doc, it's... it's not really about thinking I could have saved him if I just did *this* or *that*. It's... it's just this constant, heavy mix of guilt and anger that just sits right here in my chest all day long. It's the worst when I look at my nephew. I just look at him and think... he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. 

We were actually watching TV last night, trying to find something on Netflix. He wanted to watch this documentary about volcanoes. I guess they're learning about Pompeii in school or something. Pompeii is wild when you think about it. Just... boom, the whole city frozen in ash. My history teacher in high school, Mr. Gable, he went to Italy one summer and brought back this little piece of pumice stone from Mount Vesuvius. He used to pass it around the classroom. Mr. Gable was a trip. He used to wear these crazy loud Hawaiian shirts every Friday, even in the dead of winter. Said it kept his spirit warm. He drove this beat-up old VW Bug, bright yellow. You could hear him coming from a mile away because the muffler was shot. He was always talking about fixing it, but I don't think he ever did. He retired my junior year and moved down to Boca. I wonder if he still drives that thing... 

But yeah, it's not about a specific thing I could have done. Just... that heavy feeling, you know? All the time.

Just the normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happened to your brother that you feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. It's just... there's this heavy mix of guilt and anger just sitting right here in my chest, all day long. It never really goes away. Especially when I look at my nephew, you know? I look at him and I just think about how he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. It's not fair to him. \n\nHe's a good kid, too. He just started playing Little League this spring. Plays shortstop, just like I did when I was his age. I had this coach, Coach Miller—guy used to chew tobacco during the games and spit it right into a styrofoam cup in the dugout. It was disgusting. But he knew baseball. Taught me how to turn a double play. We actually made it to the state championships that year. We lost in the bottom of the ninth, though. Jimmy Peterson dropped a pop fly right out in right field. Kid was devastated. His mom brought orange slices for everyone after the game, but nobody even wanted to eat them. We just sat there on the grass, staring at the dirt. But yeah... it's just this constant heavy feeling. I hate that my nephew has to deal with me being like this.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like there was something you could have done to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. I mean, I've been an electrician for years. If all my years of experience couldn't keep my own brother safe, then what good is it? I came in here because I just can't shake the thought that I'm a danger to anybody who works with me now, or even anybody who lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of the roof, I really need to get up there and clean out the gutters. They've been completely backed up since that massive storm we had a few weeks ago. The wind was so bad it blew half the leaves from the Johnson's oak tree right into my yard. Old man Johnson, he loves that tree. He spends hours out there just raking. He’s got this vintage riding mower he drives around on, painted bright green. Says he bought it at an auction down in Springfield back in '98. I went to an auction once, looking for some cheap tools, but I ended up buying this antique lamp instead. It had this stained glass shade with little blue dragonflies on it. My ex-wife absolutely hated it, said it was tacky, but I thought it gave off a really nice warm light in the hallway. It's just sitting in a box in the basement now, gathering dust. But... yeah. I just feel like I'm a hazard to everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think you could have done something to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I do. I just... I keep going over it in my head, thinking about what I could have checked, what I should have noticed. It's like a loop. Like when a record skips, you know? \n\nI used to collect vinyl, actually. Had a whole setup in my living room before I moved. I had this original pressing of *Dark Side of the Moon* that I found at a garage sale for like, five bucks. The guy selling it had no idea what he had. He was just trying to clear out his garage to make room for this massive boat he bought. It was one of those pontoon boats, the kind you take out on the lake and just sit on all day drinking beer. I went out on one of those once with my buddy Dave. Dave was a character—he always wore these ridiculous Hawaiian shirts, no matter what the weather was like. Even in December, he'd have a Hawaiian shirt on under his winter coat. He claimed he was trying to manifest a tropical vacation, but he never actually went anywhere. Just stayed right here in town, working at the hardware store. He could tell you exactly where every single screw and washer was in that place. It was amazing, really. But... yeah. I just can't help feeling like I should have known better. I should have seen it coming.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen during the incident with your brother that you feel you could have done differently?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not one specific thing, I guess. It's just the whole... the whole situation. I mean, we were working on that same panel together, and I just keep thinking... maybe if I had double-checked the breaker, or... I don't know. \n\nI was actually thinking about getting a new tool belt the other day. Mine is just falling apart. The leather is all cracked near the buckle. I bought it at this little hardware store down in Florida years ago when I went down there to help my uncle fix up his condo after a hurricane. He lived right on the coast, beautiful place, but man, the damage was unbelievable. We spent three weeks just ripping out drywall and pulling up soaked carpet. He had this neighbor, Mrs. Gable, who would bring us lemonade every afternoon at exactly 2:00 PM. She had these tiny little dogs, Pomeranians, I think? Just little balls of fluff that would run around yapping at everything. One of them actually tried to bite my boot once. Didn't even make a dent in the leather, obviously, but it was pretty funny watching this five-pound dog try to take down a steel-toe boot.
