I don't think so. I mean, I didn't actually do anything wrong, technically speaking. There wasn't a missed step or a crossed wire that I can point to and say, \"That's it, that's what I messed up.\" \n\nIt's funny you mention wires, though, because I was just looking at the wiring in my own basement last night. The guy who owned the house before me, he fancied himself a bit of a handyman, but he did a terrible job. He used wire nuts that were way too small, and half the junction boxes aren't even covered. I found an old receipt of his stuffed behind one of the panels from a diner out on Route 9. The Starlight Diner, I think it was called. They used to make this incredible cherry pie. My dad would take me there sometimes on Sunday mornings when I was a kid, right after church. He always ordered his coffee black, said anything else ruined it. He had this old Chevy truck that smelled like motor oil and peppermint, because he always kept those little red and white mints in the glove compartment. I tried to find a truck like that a few years back, just for the nostalgia, you know? Looked all over Craigslist, even drove out to a farm in Ohio to check one out, but the rust was just eating right through the floorboards. The farmer had goats climbing all over it. Goats are weird animals, the way their eyes look. But... no. There wasn't anything I did or didn't do. It just... happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your brother's death would have been prevented if you had acted differently in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... that's exactly it. That's the whole reason I came in here to talk to you. I just can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then... then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me. Or even anyone who just lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of roofs, I actually had to get mine patched up last spring. We had this crazy hail storm that just came out of nowhere. Golf ball-sized hail, just completely destroying the shingles. The guy who came out to do the estimate, he drove this old El Camino. You really don't see those around much anymore, you know? It was painted this bright, cherry red. Reminded me of my cousin Tommy's car back in high school. Tommy was obsessed with restoring classic cars, but he was honestly terrible at it. He'd take the engine apart and then just leave all the parts scattered all over his mom's driveway for months. She'd get so mad because she couldn't park her minivan. She drove one of those early 90s Dodge Caravans with the fake wood paneling on the side. We took that thing on a road trip to Mount Rushmore once when we were kids. The AC broke halfway through South Dakota, and it was like ninety-five degrees out. We had to roll all the windows down, and the wind was just howling so loud you couldn't even hear the radio. My aunt kept trying to play these John Denver cassettes... \n\nBut, I mean, yeah. If my own experience wasn't enough to protect him, I just feel like I'm a walking hazard to everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific you did or didn't do during the incident with your brother that you think might have contributed to the outcome?\n\nPatient: No, there wasn't... I mean, I've racked my brain a million times, and I can't think of one single thing I actually did wrong. Or forgot to do. It's just... the whole thing. \n\nI was actually just looking at the tools I had with me that day. They're still sitting in the garage in this old metal toolbox my grandfather gave me. He was a machinist down in Philly for, like, forty years. Tough guy. He had this crazy scar on his forearm from where a piece of sheet metal caught him. He used to tell me he got it fighting a bear in the Poconos. I believed him until I was about ten. We used to go up to the Poconos every summer, actually, to this cabin near Lake Wallenpaupack. The mosquitoes up there were just massive. We used to burn those citronella candles, the ones in the little tin buckets, but they never really worked. My cousin got bit so bad once his eye swelled completely shut. He looked like he'd gone a few rounds with Mike Tyson. Tyson had that fight... when was it, '97? The one where he bit Holyfield's ear? Man, I remember watching that on pay-per-view at my buddy Dave's house. Dave made these incredible nachos with, like, three different kinds of cheese and jalapeños he grew in his own backyard. He had a real green thumb, Dave did. Grew tomatoes the size of softballs. \n\nBut no, honestly, there wasn't any specific mistake I made. I just... I don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because of something you did or didn't do during the incident with your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... it's not even about a specific mistake, like I said. It's just this... this heavy mix of guilt and anger that just sits right here in my chest all day long. Especially when I look at my nephew. I look at him and I just think... he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. \n\nWe were actually trying to play catch in the yard the other day. I bought him this new baseball glove, a Rawlings. Real nice leather, took forever to break in. I used to use shaving cream to break in my gloves when I was in Little League. Shaving cream and then you'd wrap it in rubber bands and stick it under your mattress for a week. My mom hated it because it made the whole room smell like Barbasol. She always bought the cheap brand, never the gel stuff. She was super frugal like that, you know? Like, she would save those little plastic bread ties and use them for everything. Organizing cords, tying up tomato plants... She had this huge garden out back. Grew the biggest zucchini I've ever seen. We had to eat zucchini bread, zucchini boats, fried zucchini, all summer long. I can't even look at a zucchini now without feeling slightly nauseous. But anyway, yeah, the guilt is just... it's just always there, weighing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think there was something you should have done differently when you were working with your brother?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's like I was just saying a minute ago... it really isn't about some specific move I should have made differently, or a wire I should've checked twice. It's just this... this constant, heavy mix of guilt and anger. It just sits right here in my chest all day long. And it gets so much worse when I look at my nephew, knowing he lost his dad and now he's just... stuck with me instead. \n\nI actually had to take him to his dentist appointment on Tuesday. Dr. Henderson over on Elm Street. His office has this huge fish tank in the waiting room. Saltwater tank. Got these little clownfish in it, some tangs. I always wanted a saltwater tank, but I heard they're a total nightmare to keep clean. The pH balance gets messed up and everything just dies overnight. My buddy Greg had one, and he spent like two grand on live coral alone, just for his water heater to break while he was on vacation in Florida. He went down to Key West for a bachelor party. They rented this pontoon boat and ended up getting stranded on a sandbar for like six hours. Said they ran out of beer in the first hour and just had to sit there baking in the sun until the tide came back in. He got a sunburn so bad his shoulders were peeling for a month... \n\nBut anyway. Yeah. It's just that heavy feeling all the time. Not really about one specific thing I forgot to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think there was something you could have done to save your brother?\n\nPatient: Like I've been saying, doc, it's... it's not really about thinking I could have saved him if I just did *this* or *that*. Plays shortstop, just like I did when I was his age. I had this coach, Coach Miller—guy used to chew tobacco during the games and spit it right into a styrofoam cup in the dugout. It was disgusting. But he knew baseball. Taught me how to turn a double play. We actually made it to the state championships that year. We lost in the bottom of the ninth, though. Jimmy Peterson dropped a pop fly right out in right field. Kid was devastated. His mom brought orange slices for everyone after the game, but nobody even wanted to eat them. We just sat there on the grass, staring at the dirt. But yeah... it's just this constant heavy feeling. I hate that my nephew has to deal with me being like this.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like there was something you could have done to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. I mean, I've been an electrician for years. If all my years of experience couldn't keep my own brother safe, then what good is it? I came in here because I just can't shake the thought that I'm a danger to anybody who works with me now, or even anybody who lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of the roof, I really need to get up there and clean out the gutters. They've been completely backed up since that massive storm we had a few weeks ago. The wind was so bad it blew half the leaves from the Johnson's oak tree right into my yard. Old man Johnson, he loves that tree. He spends hours out there just raking. He’s got this vintage riding mower he drives around on, painted bright green. Says he bought it at an auction down in Springfield back in '98. I went to an auction once, looking for some cheap tools, but I ended up buying this antique lamp instead. It had this stained glass shade with little blue dragonflies on it. My ex-wife absolutely hated it, said it was tacky, but I thought it gave off a really nice warm light in the hallway. It's just sitting in a box in the basement now, gathering dust. But... yeah. I just feel like I'm a hazard to everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think you could have done something to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I do. I just... I keep going over it in my head, thinking about what I could have checked, what I should have noticed. It's like a loop. Like when a record skips, you know? \n\nI used to collect vinyl, actually. Had a whole setup in my living room before I moved. I had this original pressing of *Dark Side of the Moon* that I found at a garage sale for like, five bucks. The guy selling it had no idea what he had. He was just trying to clear out his garage to make room for this massive boat he bought. It was one of those pontoon boats, the kind you take out on the lake and just sit on all day drinking beer. I went out on one of those once with my buddy Dave. Dave was a character—he always wore these ridiculous Hawaiian shirts, no matter what the weather was like. Even in December, he'd have a Hawaiian shirt on under his winter coat. He claimed he was trying to manifest a tropical vacation, but he never actually went anywhere. Just stayed right here in town, working at the hardware store. He could tell you exactly where every single screw and washer was in that place. It was amazing, really. But... yeah. I just can't help feeling like I should have known better. I should have seen it coming.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen during the incident with your brother that you feel you could have done differently?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not one specific thing, I guess. It's just the whole... the whole situation. I mean, we were working on that same panel together, and I just keep thinking... maybe if I had double-checked the breaker, or... I don't know. \n\nI was actually thinking about getting a new tool belt the other day. Mine is just falling apart. The leather is all cracked near the buckle. I bought it at this little hardware store down in Florida years ago when I went down there to help my uncle fix up his condo after a hurricane. He lived right on the coast, beautiful place, but man, the damage was unbelievable. We spent three weeks just ripping out drywall and pulling up soaked carpet. He had this neighbor, Mrs. Gable, who would bring us lemonade every afternoon at exactly 2:00 PM. She had these tiny little dogs, Pomeranians, I think? Just little balls of fluff that would run around yapping at everything. One of them actually tried to bite my boot once. Didn't even make a dent in the leather, obviously, but it was pretty funny watching this five-pound dog try to take down a steel-toe boot. I used to buy Red Wings, but lately I've been looking at those Thorogoods. Supposed to be better for your arches, and God knows my feet are killing me by the end of the day. But anyway... no, there wasn't like, one exact moment where I did something wrong. It's just... the whole thing just sits heavy on me.\n\nDoctor: Did you do something or fail to do something during the incident with your brother that you now wish you had handled differently?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I didn't actually do anything wrong, technically speaking. There wasn't a missed step or a crossed wire that I can point to and say, \"That's it, that's what I messed up.\" \n\nIt's funny you mention wires, though, because I was just looking at the wiring in my own basement last night. The guy who owned the house before me, he fancied himself a bit of a handyman, but he did a terrible job. He used wire nuts that were way too small, and half the junction boxes aren't even covered. I found an old receipt of his stuffed behind one of the panels from a diner out on Route 9. The Starlight Diner, I think it was called. They used to make this incredible cherry pie. My dad would take me there sometimes on Sunday mornings when I was a kid, right after church. He always ordered his coffee black, said anything else ruined it. He had this old Chevy truck that smelled like motor oil and peppermint, because he always kept those little red and white mints in the glove compartment. I tried to find a truck like that a few years back, just for the nostalgia, you know? Looked all over Craigslist, even drove out to a farm in Ohio to check one out, but the rust was just eating right through the floorboards. The farmer had goats climbing all over it. Goats are weird animals, the way their eyes look. But... no. There wasn't anything I did or didn't do. It just... happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your brother's death would have been prevented if you had acted differently in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... that's exactly it. That's the whole reason I came in here to talk to you. I just can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then... then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me. Or even anyone who just lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of roofs, I actually had to get mine patched up last spring. We had this crazy hail storm that just came out of nowhere. Golf ball-sized hail, just completely destroying the shingles. The guy who came out to do the estimate, he drove this old El Camino. You really don't see those around much anymore, you know? It was painted this bright, cherry red. Reminded me of my cousin Tommy's car back in high school. Tommy was obsessed with restoring classic cars, but he was honestly terrible at it. He'd take the engine apart and then just leave all the parts scattered all over his mom's driveway for months. She'd get so mad because she couldn't park her minivan. She drove one of those early 90s Dodge Caravans with the fake wood paneling on the side. We took that thing on a road trip to Mount Rushmore once when we were kids. The AC broke halfway through South Dakota, and it was like ninety-five degrees out. We had to roll all the windows down, and the wind was just howling so loud you couldn't even hear the radio. My aunt kept trying to play these John Denver cassettes... \n\nBut, I mean, yeah. If my own experience wasn't enough to protect him, I just feel like I'm a walking hazard to everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific you did or didn't do during the incident with your brother that you think might have contributed to the outcome?\n\nPatient: No, there wasn't... I mean, I've racked my brain a million times, and I can't think of one single thing I actually did wrong. Or forgot to do. It's just... the whole thing. \n\nI was actually just looking at the tools I had with me that day. They're still sitting in the garage in this old metal toolbox my grandfather gave me. He was a machinist down in Philly for, like, forty years. Tough guy. He had this crazy scar on his forearm from where a piece of sheet metal caught him. He used to tell me he got it fighting a bear in the Poconos. I believed him until I was about ten. We used to go up to the Poconos every summer, actually, to this cabin near Lake Wallenpaupack. The mosquitoes up there were just massive. We used to burn those citronella candles, the ones in the little tin buckets, but they never really worked. My cousin got bit so bad once his eye swelled completely shut. He looked like he'd gone a few rounds with Mike Tyson. Tyson had that fight... when was it, '97? The one where he bit Holyfield's ear? Man, I remember watching that on pay-per-view at my buddy Dave's house. Dave made these incredible nachos with, like, three different kinds of cheese and jalapeños he grew in his own backyard. He had a real green thumb, Dave did. Grew tomatoes the size of softballs. \n\nBut no, honestly, there wasn't any specific mistake I made. I just... I don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because of something you did or didn't do during the incident with your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... it's not even about a specific mistake, like I said. It's just this... this heavy mix of guilt and anger that just sits right here in my chest all day long. Especially when I look at my nephew. I look at him and I just think... he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. \n\nWe were actually trying to play catch in the yard the other day. I bought him this new baseball glove, a Rawlings. Real nice leather, took forever to break in. I used to use shaving cream to break in my gloves when I was in Little League. Shaving cream and then you'd wrap it in rubber bands and stick it under your mattress for a week. My mom hated it because it made the whole room smell like Barbasol. She always bought the cheap brand, never the gel stuff. She was super frugal like that, you know? Like, she would save those little plastic bread ties and use them for everything. Organizing cords, tying up tomato plants... She had this huge garden out back. Grew the biggest zucchini I've ever seen. We had to eat zucchini bread, zucchini boats, fried zucchini, all summer long. I can't even look at a zucchini now without feeling slightly nauseous. But anyway, yeah, the guilt is just... it's just always there, weighing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think there was something you should have done differently when you were working with your brother?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's like I was just saying a minute ago... it really isn't about some specific move I should have made differently, or a wire I should've checked twice. It's just this... this constant, heavy mix of guilt and anger. It just sits right here in my chest all day long. And it gets so much worse when I look at my nephew, knowing he lost his dad and now he's just... stuck with me instead. \n\nI actually had to take him to his dentist appointment on Tuesday. Dr. Henderson over on Elm Street. His office has this huge fish tank in the waiting room. Saltwater tank. Got these little clownfish in it, some tangs. I always wanted a saltwater tank, but I heard they're a total nightmare to keep clean. The pH balance gets messed up and everything just dies overnight. My buddy Greg had one, and he spent like two grand on live coral alone, just for his water heater to break while he was on vacation in Florida. He went down to Key West for a bachelor party. They rented this pontoon boat and ended up getting stranded on a sandbar for like six hours. Said they ran out of beer in the first hour and just had to sit there baking in the sun until the tide came back in. He got a sunburn so bad his shoulders were peeling for a month... \n\nBut anyway. Yeah. It's just that heavy feeling all the time. Not really about one specific thing I forgot to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think there was something you could have done to save your brother?\n\nPatient: Like I've been saying, doc, it's... it's not really about thinking I could have saved him if I just did *this* or *that*. It's... it's just this constant, heavy mix of guilt and anger that just sits right here in my chest all day long. It's the worst when I look at my nephew. I just look at him and think... he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. \n\nWe were actually watching TV last night, trying to find something on Netflix. He drove this beat-up old VW Bug, bright yellow. You could hear him coming from a mile away because the muffler was shot. He was always talking about fixing it, but I don't think he ever did. He retired my junior year and moved down to Boca. I wonder if he still drives that thing... \n\nBut yeah, it's not about a specific thing I could have done. Just... that heavy feeling, you know? All the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, I feel like a broken record here, but... it's really not about a specific mistake on the job. It's just... like I keep saying, it's this heavy mix of guilt and anger that just sits right here in my chest all day long. Especially when I look at my nephew. I look at him and I just think... he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. 

I was making him breakfast this morning. Just some scrambled eggs and toast. He likes his toast practically burnt, which I don't get. Black as coal. Reminds me of this diner we used to go to when we were kids, off Route 9. The Silver Spoon. They had this cook, Sal, who always burnt the bacon. Swore it was the only way to eat it. Sal was a character. He had this anchor tattoo on his forearm that looked more like a squashed spider because the ink bled so bad over the years. He told us he got it in the Navy, stationed out in San Diego. San Diego is supposed to be beautiful. Perfect weather all year round. Seventy-two and sunny. I've always wanted to go out west, maybe see the Grand Canyon or something. Take one of those mule rides down to the bottom. Though I heard those mules can be pretty stubborn. A guy I used to work with, Dave, he went to the Grand Canyon and dropped his phone right over the edge trying to take a selfie. Brand new iPhone, too. He was so mad he didn't speak to his wife for the rest of the trip, like it was her fault he had slippery hands...

But yeah, anyway... it's just that same heavy feeling. Not about one thing I did wrong. Just... always there.

Jimmy Peterson dropped a pop fly right out in right field. Kid was devastated. His mom brought orange slices for everyone after the game, but nobody even wanted to eat them. We just sat there on the grass, staring at the dirt. But yeah... it's just this constant heavy feeling. I hate that my nephew has to deal with me being like this.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like there was something you could have done to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. I mean, I've been an electrician for years. If all my years of experience couldn't keep my own brother safe, then what good is it? I came in here because I just can't shake the thought that I'm a danger to anybody who works with me now, or even anybody who lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of the roof, I really need to get up there and clean out the gutters. They've been completely backed up since that massive storm we had a few weeks ago. The wind was so bad it blew half the leaves from the Johnson's oak tree right into my yard. Old man Johnson, he loves that tree. He spends hours out there just raking. He’s got this vintage riding mower he drives around on, painted bright green. Says he bought it at an auction down in Springfield back in '98. I went to an auction once, looking for some cheap tools, but I ended up buying this antique lamp instead. It had this stained glass shade with little blue dragonflies on it. My ex-wife absolutely hated it, said it was tacky, but I thought it gave off a really nice warm light in the hallway. It's just sitting in a box in the basement now, gathering dust. But... yeah. I just feel like I'm a hazard to everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think you could have done something to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I do. I just... I keep going over it in my head, thinking about what I could have checked, what I should have noticed. It's like a loop. Like when a record skips, you know? \n\nI used to collect vinyl, actually. Had a whole setup in my living room before I moved. I had this original pressing of *Dark Side of the Moon* that I found at a garage sale for like, five bucks. The guy selling it had no idea what he had. He was just trying to clear out his garage to make room for this massive boat he bought. It was one of those pontoon boats, the kind you take out on the lake and just sit on all day drinking beer. I went out on one of those once with my buddy Dave. Dave was a character—he always wore these ridiculous Hawaiian shirts, no matter what the weather was like. Even in December, he'd have a Hawaiian shirt on under his winter coat. He claimed he was trying to manifest a tropical vacation, but he never actually went anywhere. Just stayed right here in town, working at the hardware store. He could tell you exactly where every single screw and washer was in that place. It was amazing, really. But... yeah. I just can't help feeling like I should have known better. I should have seen it coming.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen during the incident with your brother that you feel you could have done differently?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not one specific thing, I guess. It's just the whole... the whole situation. I mean, we were working on that same panel together, and I just keep thinking... maybe if I had double-checked the breaker, or... I don't know. \n\nI was actually thinking about getting a new tool belt the other day. Mine is just falling apart. The leather is all cracked near the buckle. I bought it at this little hardware store down in Florida years ago when I went down there to help my uncle fix up his condo after a hurricane. He lived right on the coast, beautiful place, but man, the damage was unbelievable. We spent three weeks just ripping out drywall and pulling up soaked carpet. He had this neighbor, Mrs. Gable, who would bring us lemonade every afternoon at exactly 2:00 PM. She had these tiny little dogs, Pomeranians, I think? Just little balls of fluff that would run around yapping at everything. One of them actually tried to bite my boot once. Didn't even make a dent in the leather, obviously, but it was pretty funny watching this five-pound dog try to take down a steel-toe boot. I used to buy Red Wings, but lately I've been looking at those Thorogoods. Supposed to be better for your arches, and God knows my feet are killing me by the end of the day. But anyway... no, there wasn't like, one exact moment where I did something wrong. It's just... the whole thing just sits heavy on me.\n\nDoctor: Did you do something or fail to do something during the incident with your brother that you now wish you had handled differently?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I didn't actually do anything wrong, technically speaking. There wasn't a missed step or a crossed wire that I can point to and say, \"That's it, that's what I messed up.\" \n\nIt's funny you mention wires, though, because I was just looking at the wiring in my own basement last night. The guy who owned the house before me, he fancied himself a bit of a handyman, but he did a terrible job. He used wire nuts that were way too small, and half the junction boxes aren't even covered. I found an old receipt of his stuffed behind one of the panels from a diner out on Route 9. The Starlight Diner, I think it was called. They used to make this incredible cherry pie. My dad would take me there sometimes on Sunday mornings when I was a kid, right after church. He always ordered his coffee black, said anything else ruined it. He had this old Chevy truck that smelled like motor oil and peppermint, because he always kept those little red and white mints in the glove compartment. I tried to find a truck like that a few years back, just for the nostalgia, you know? Looked all over Craigslist, even drove out to a farm in Ohio to check one out, but the rust was just eating right through the floorboards. The farmer had goats climbing all over it. Goats are weird animals, the way their eyes look. But... no. There wasn't anything I did or didn't do. It just... happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your brother's death would have been prevented if you had acted differently in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... that's exactly it. That's the whole reason I came in here to talk to you. I just can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then... then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me. Or even anyone who just lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of roofs, I actually had to get mine patched up last spring. We had this crazy hail storm that just came out of nowhere. Golf ball-sized hail, just completely destroying the shingles. The guy who came out to do the estimate, he drove this old El Camino. You really don't see those around much anymore, you know? It was painted this bright, cherry red. Reminded me of my cousin Tommy's car back in high school. Tommy was obsessed with restoring classic cars, but he was honestly terrible at it. He'd take the engine apart and then just leave all the parts scattered all over his mom's driveway for months. She'd get so mad because she couldn't park her minivan. She drove one of those early 90s Dodge Caravans with the fake wood paneling on the side. We took that thing on a road trip to Mount Rushmore once when we were kids. The AC broke halfway through South Dakota, and it was like ninety-five degrees out. We had to roll all the windows down, and the wind was just howling so loud you couldn't even hear the radio. My aunt kept trying to play these John Denver cassettes... \n\nBut, I mean, yeah. If my own experience wasn't enough to protect him, I just feel like I'm a walking hazard to everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific you did or didn't do during the incident with your brother that you think might have contributed to the outcome?\n\nPatient: No, there wasn't... I mean, I've racked my brain a million times, and I can't think of one single thing I actually did wrong. Or forgot to do. It's just... the whole thing. \n\nI was actually just looking at the tools I had with me that day. They're still sitting in the garage in this old metal toolbox my grandfather gave me. He was a machinist down in Philly for, like, forty years. Tough guy. He had this crazy scar on his forearm from where a piece of sheet metal caught him. He used to tell me he got it fighting a bear in the Poconos. I believed him until I was about ten. We used to go up to the Poconos every summer, actually, to this cabin near Lake Wallenpaupack. The mosquitoes up there were just massive. We used to burn those citronella candles, the ones in the little tin buckets, but they never really worked. My cousin got bit so bad once his eye swelled completely shut. He looked like he'd gone a few rounds with Mike Tyson. Tyson had that fight... when was it, '97? The one where he bit Holyfield's ear? Man, I remember watching that on pay-per-view at my buddy Dave's house. Dave made these incredible nachos with, like, three different kinds of cheese and jalapeños he grew in his own backyard. He had a real green thumb, Dave did. Grew tomatoes the size of softballs. \n\nBut no, honestly, there wasn't any specific mistake I made. I just... I don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because of something you did or didn't do during the incident with your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... it's not even about a specific mistake, like I said. It's just this... this heavy mix of guilt and anger that just sits right here in my chest all day long. Especially when I look at my nephew. I look at him and I just think... he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. \n\nWe were actually trying to play catch in the yard the other day. I bought him this new baseball glove, a Rawlings. Real nice leather, took forever to break in. I used to use shaving cream to break in my gloves when I was in Little League. Shaving cream and then you'd wrap it in rubber bands and stick it under your mattress for a week. My mom hated it because it made the whole room smell like Barbasol. She always bought the cheap brand, never the gel stuff. She was super frugal like that, you know? Like, she would save those little plastic bread ties and use them for everything. Organizing cords, tying up tomato plants... She had this huge garden out back. Grew the biggest zucchini I've ever seen. We had to eat zucchini bread, zucchini boats, fried zucchini, all summer long. I can't even look at a zucchini now without feeling slightly nauseous. But anyway, yeah, the guilt is just... it's just always there, weighing me down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think there was something you should have done differently when you were working with your brother?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's like I was just saying a minute ago... it really isn't about some specific move I should have made differently, or a wire I should've checked twice. It's just this... this constant, heavy mix of guilt and anger. It just sits right here in my chest all day long. And it gets so much worse when I look at my nephew, knowing he lost his dad and now he's just... stuck with me instead. \n\nI actually had to take him to his dentist appointment on Tuesday. Dr. Henderson over on Elm Street. His office has this huge fish tank in the waiting room. Saltwater tank. Got these little clownfish in it, some tangs. I always wanted a saltwater tank, but I heard they're a total nightmare to keep clean. The pH balance gets messed up and everything just dies overnight. My buddy Greg had one, and he spent like two grand on live coral alone, just for his water heater to break while he was on vacation in Florida. He went down to Key West for a bachelor party. They rented this pontoon boat and ended up getting stranded on a sandbar for like six hours. Said they ran out of beer in the first hour and just had to sit there baking in the sun until the tide came back in. He got a sunburn so bad his shoulders were peeling for a month... \n\nBut anyway. Yeah. It's just that heavy feeling all the time. Not really about one specific thing I forgot to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think there was something you could have done to save your brother?\n\nPatient: Like I've been saying, doc, it's... it's not really about thinking I could have saved him if I just did *this* or *that*. It's... it's just this constant, heavy mix of guilt and anger that just sits right here in my chest all day long. It's the worst when I look at my nephew. I just look at him and think... he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. \n\nWe were actually watching TV last night, trying to find something on Netflix. He wanted to watch this documentary about volcanoes. I guess they're learning about Pompeii in school or something. Pompeii is wild when you think about it. Just... boom, the whole city frozen in ash. My history teacher in high school, Mr. Gable, he went to Italy one summer and brought back this little piece of pumice stone from Mount Vesuvius. He used to pass it around the classroom. Mr. Gable was a trip. He used to wear these crazy loud Hawaiian shirts every Friday, even in the dead of winter. Said it kept his spirit warm. He drove this beat-up old VW Bug, bright yellow. You could hear him coming from a mile away because the muffler was shot. He was always talking about fixing it, but I don't think he ever did. He retired my junior year and moved down to Boca. I wonder if he still drives that thing... \n\nBut yeah, it's not about a specific thing I could have done. Just... that heavy feeling, you know? All the time.\n\nDoctor: Is your guilt related to something you think you should or shouldn't have done when working with your brother?\n\nPatient: Doc, I feel like a broken record here, but... it's really not about a specific mistake on the job. It's just... like I keep saying, it's this heavy mix of guilt and anger that just sits right here in my chest all day long. Especially when I look at my nephew. I look at him and I just think... he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. \n\nI was making him breakfast this morning. Just some scrambled eggs and toast. He likes his toast practically burnt, which I don't get. Black as coal. Reminds me of this diner we used to go to when we were kids, off Route 9. The Silver Spoon. They had this cook, Sal, who always burnt the bacon. Swore it was the only way to eat it. Sal was a character. He had this anchor tattoo on his forearm that looked more like a squashed spider because the ink bled so bad over the years. He told us he got it in the Navy, stationed out in San Diego. San Diego is supposed to be beautiful. Perfect weather all year round. Seventy-two and sunny. I've always wanted to go out west, maybe see the Grand Canyon or something. Take one of those mule rides down to the bottom. Though I heard those mules can be pretty stubborn. A guy I used to work with, Dave, he went to the Grand Canyon and dropped his phone right over the edge trying to take a selfie. Brand new iPhone, too. He was so mad he didn't speak to his wife for the rest of the trip, like it was her fault he had slippery hands...\n\nBut yeah, anyway... it's just that same heavy feeling. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, doc, I don't know if "responsible" is the right word. I mean, I don't think it was my fault, like I didn't cause the accident. But I still just have that... that heavy feeling. Guilt, anger, whatever it is. It's just there. Especially with the kid. 

I took him to the park yesterday to just get out of the house. The one over by the lake, with the big wooden playground. They just put down all new mulch. Smelled like cedar. Reminded me of this cedar chest my grandma used to have at the foot of her bed. She kept all her old quilts in it. Man, she could quilt. She made this one for me when I graduated high school, had all these little squares made out of my old flannel shirts. I still have it in the hall closet. My dog, Buster, he used to love sleeping on that thing. Buster was a good boy. Golden retriever mix. Found him wandering around a construction site when I was an apprentice. He was just a puppy, chewing on a piece of drywall. Took him home, and he lived to be fourteen. Fourteen is a long time for a big dog. His hips went out at the end, though. Had to carry him up and down the porch stairs... 

But yeah, I don't know if I feel responsible, exactly. I just... I just feel bad. All the time.

It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing anything over and over to try and cope with these thoughts about your brother and your work?\n\nPatient: Over and over? I mean, I don't... I don't think so. I just try to keep my head down, you know? Just get through the day without thinking about it. \n\nI did start going to this diner down the street from my house, though. Just to get out, I guess. They make this incredible meatloaf on Thursdays. It's got this brown gravy that tastes exactly like the kind my grandma used to make. She was a character, my grandma. She had this tiny little kitchen, and she'd cook for, like, twenty people every Thanksgiving. I don't know how she fit all those pots on the stove. And she always had this old radio playing—AM station, crackling the whole time. She said the static gave the food flavor. I don't know about that, but man, she made a good pie. Cherry, usually. But yeah, I don't really do anything over and over. Just... just trying to get by.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing anything repeatedly to try and prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Prevent something bad? No, not really. I mean, I check the locks on the doors at night, but everybody does that, right? You just want to make sure the house is secure. \n\nActually, speaking of the house, I've been meaning to fix the weather stripping on the back door. It's been letting in this terrible draft lately. The other night, I swear it was howling. Sounded just like this stray dog that used to hang around my old apartment building back when I was in my twenties. We called him Buster. He was this scruffy terrier mix, always getting into the trash. One time, he dragged a whole pizza box up three flights of stairs and just left it right in front of my neighbor's door. Mrs. Higgins, I think her name was. She was furious. She always wore these bright pink curlers in her hair, even in the middle of the afternoon. She threatened to call animal control, but I think she secretly liked him. She used to leave little bowls of milk out on the fire escape. But no, I don't do anything over and over to stop bad things. Just the normal stuff.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happened to your brother that you feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. It's just... there's this heavy mix of guilt and anger just sitting right here in my chest, all day long. It never really goes away. Especially when I look at my nephew, you know? I look at him and I just think about how he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. It's not fair to him. \n\nHe's a good kid, too. He just started playing Little League this spring. Plays shortstop, just like I did when I was his age. I had this coach, Coach Miller—guy used to chew tobacco during the games and spit it right into a styrofoam cup in the dugout. It was disgusting. But he knew baseball. Taught me how to turn a double play. We actually made it to the state championships that year. We lost in the bottom of the ninth, though. Jimmy Peterson dropped a pop fly right out in right field. Kid was devastated. His mom brought orange slices for everyone after the game, but nobody even wanted to eat them. We just sat there on the grass, staring at the dirt. But yeah... it's just this constant heavy feeling. I hate that my nephew has to deal with me being like this.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like there was something you could have done to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. I mean, I've been an electrician for years. If all my years of experience couldn't keep my own brother safe, then what good is it? I came in here because I just can't shake the thought that I'm a danger to anybody who works with me now, or even anybody who lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of the roof, I really need to get up there and clean out the gutters. They've been completely backed up since that massive storm we had a few weeks ago. The wind was so bad it blew half the leaves from the Johnson's oak tree right into my yard. Old man Johnson, he loves that tree. He spends hours out there just raking. He’s got this vintage riding mower he drives around on, painted bright green. Says he bought it at an auction down in Springfield back in '98. I went to an auction once, looking for some cheap tools, but I ended up buying this antique lamp instead. It had this stained glass shade with little blue dragonflies on it. My ex-wife absolutely hated it, said it was tacky, but I thought it gave off a really nice warm light in the hallway. It's just sitting in a box in the basement now, gathering dust. But... yeah. I just feel like I'm a hazard to everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think you could have done something to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I do. I just... I keep going over it in my head, thinking about what I could have checked, what I should have noticed. It's like a loop. Like when a record skips, you know? \n\nI used to collect vinyl, actually. Had a whole setup in my living room before I moved. I had this original pressing of *Dark Side of the Moon* that I found at a garage sale for like, five bucks. The guy selling it had no idea what he had. He was just trying to clear out his garage to make room for this massive boat he bought. It was one of those pontoon boats, the kind you take out on the lake and just sit on all day drinking beer. I went out on one of those once with my buddy Dave. Dave was a character—he always wore these ridiculous Hawaiian shirts, no matter what the weather was like. Even in December, he'd have a Hawaiian shirt on under his winter coat. He claimed he was trying to manifest a tropical vacation, but he never actually went anywhere. Just stayed right here in town, working at the hardware store. He could tell you exactly where every single screw and washer was in that place. It was amazing, really. But... yeah. I just can't help feeling like I should have known better. I should have seen it coming.\n\nDoctor: Did something specific happen during the incident with your brother that you feel you could have done differently?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not one specific thing, I guess. It's just the whole... the whole situation. I mean, we were working on that same panel together, and I just keep thinking... maybe if I had double-checked the breaker, or... I don't know. \n\nI was actually thinking about getting a new tool belt the other day. Mine is just falling apart. The leather is all cracked near the buckle. I bought it at this little hardware store down in Florida years ago when I went down there to help my uncle fix up his condo after a hurricane. He lived right on the coast, beautiful place, but man, the damage was unbelievable. We spent three weeks just ripping out drywall and pulling up soaked carpet. He had this neighbor, Mrs. Gable, who would bring us lemonade every afternoon at exactly 2:00 PM. She had these tiny little dogs, Pomeranians, I think? Just little balls of fluff that would run around yapping at everything. One of them actually tried to bite my boot once. Didn't even make a dent in the leather, obviously, but it was pretty funny watching this five-pound dog try to take down a steel-toe boot. I used to buy Red Wings, but lately I've been looking at those Thorogoods. Supposed to be better for your arches, and God knows my feet are killing me by the end of the day. But anyway... no, there wasn't like, one exact moment where I did something wrong. It's just... the whole thing just sits heavy on me.\n\nDoctor: Did you do something or fail to do something during the incident with your brother that you now wish you had handled differently?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. I mean, I didn't actually do anything wrong, technically speaking. There wasn't a missed step or a crossed wire that I can point to and say, \"That's it, that's what I messed up.\" \n\nIt's funny you mention wires, though, because I was just looking at the wiring in my own basement last night. The guy who owned the house before me, he fancied himself a bit of a handyman, but he did a terrible job. He used wire nuts that were way too small, and half the junction boxes aren't even covered. I found an old receipt of his stuffed behind one of the panels from a diner out on Route 9. The Starlight Diner, I think it was called. They used to make this incredible cherry pie. My dad would take me there sometimes on Sunday mornings when I was a kid, right after church. He always ordered his coffee black, said anything else ruined it. He had this old Chevy truck that smelled like motor oil and peppermint, because he always kept those little red and white mints in the glove compartment. I tried to find a truck like that a few years back, just for the nostalgia, you know? Looked all over Craigslist, even drove out to a farm in Ohio to check one out, but the rust was just eating right through the floorboards. The farmer had goats climbing all over it. Goats are weird animals, the way their eyes look. But... no. There wasn't anything I did or didn't do. It just... happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your brother's death would have been prevented if you had acted differently in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... that's exactly it. That's the whole reason I came in here to talk to you. I just can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then... then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me. Or even anyone who just lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of roofs, I actually had to get mine patched up last spring. We had this crazy hail storm that just came out of nowhere. Golf ball-sized hail, just completely destroying the shingles. The guy who came out to do the estimate, he drove this old El Camino. You really don't see those around much anymore, you know? It was painted this bright, cherry red. Reminded me of my cousin Tommy's car back in high school. Tommy was obsessed with restoring classic cars, but he was honestly terrible at it. He'd take the engine apart and then just leave all the parts scattered all over his mom's driveway for months. She'd get so mad because she couldn't park her minivan. She drove one of those early 90s Dodge Caravans with the fake wood paneling on the side. We took that thing on a road trip to Mount Rushmore once when we were kids. The AC broke halfway through South Dakota, and it was like ninety-five degrees out. We had to roll all the windows down, and the wind was just howling so loud you couldn't even hear the radio. My aunt kept trying to play these John Denver cassettes... \n\nBut, I mean, yeah. If my own experience wasn't enough to protect him, I just feel like I'm a walking hazard to everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific you did or didn't do during the incident with your brother that you think might have contributed to the outcome?\n\nPatient: No, there wasn't... I mean, I've racked my brain a million times, and I can't think of one single thing I actually did wrong. Or forgot to do. It's just... the whole thing. \n\nI was actually just looking at the tools I had with me that day. They're still sitting in the garage in this old metal toolbox my grandfather gave me. He was a machinist down in Philly for, like, forty years. Tough guy. He had this crazy scar on his forearm from where a piece of sheet metal caught him. He used to tell me he got it fighting a bear in the Poconos. I believed him until I was about ten. We used to go up to the Poconos every summer, actually, to this cabin near Lake Wallenpaupack. The mosquitoes up there were just massive. We used to burn those citronella candles, the ones in the little tin buckets, but they never really worked. My cousin got bit so bad once his eye swelled completely shut. He looked like he'd gone a few rounds with Mike Tyson. Tyson had that fight... when was it, '97? The one where he bit Holyfield's ear? Man, I remember watching that on pay-per-view at my buddy Dave's house. Dave made these incredible nachos with, like, three different kinds of cheese and jalapeños he grew in his own backyard. He had a real green thumb, Dave did. Grew tomatoes the size of softballs. \n\nBut no, honestly, there wasn't any specific mistake I made. I just... I don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because of something you did or didn't do during the incident with your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... it's not even about a specific mistake, like I said. I look at him and I just think... he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. \n\nWe were actually trying to play catch in the yard the other day. My buddy Greg had one, and he spent like two grand on live coral alone, just for his water heater to break while he was on vacation in Florida. He went down to Key West for a bachelor party. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it's not that I think it wouldn't have happened if I wasn't there... it's more like... I came in here because I can't shake this thought. I've been an electrician for twenty years, right? I know what I'm doing. But if all those years of experience couldn't keep my own brother safe on a job site, then what good am I? I just keep thinking I'm a danger to anybody who works with me, or even lives under my roof. 

Which is why I've been trying to fix up the house, make sure everything is perfect. I was up in the attic yesterday checking the wiring, just to be safe. It gets so hot up there. Reminded me of this summer I spent roofing with my uncle down in Florida. Man, the humidity down there is something else. You step outside and it's like breathing through a wet washcloth. My uncle, he loved it though. Said it was good for his joints. He had this old pontoon boat we used to take out on the weekends. We'd just sit out there for hours, trying to catch snapper. Never caught much, mostly just got sunburned. He had this cooler that he painted with the Miami Dolphins logo. Huge Dolphins fan. Had the Dan Marino jersey and everything. I think they went undefeated one year back in the seventies, right? The '72 Dolphins. Only team to ever do it. That's a crazy record to hold on to for that long. Wonder if anyone will ever beat it... 

But yeah, it's just... if I couldn't protect him, I feel like I can't protect anyone. It's just a bad feeling.

I don't really think about it like that. It's not like I sit awake thinking, 'Oh, if I had just flipped that breaker' or 'if I used a different gauge wire.' I know the job was done by the book. I've had the same set of Klein tools for... man, it's gotta be fifteen years. Bought 'em at this little mom-and-pop hardware store on 4th Street. Old man Higgins ran the place. The whole store smelled like motor oil and sawdust, you know? The real deal. Not like these big box stores today where half the kids working there don't know a Phillips from a flathead. 

Higgins had this massive vintage Coca-Cola sign behind the counter. Said it was from the 1950s. The red paint was all chipped on the edges, but it was a classic. People used to offer him hundreds of bucks for it, but he wouldn't budge. He told me his wife bought it at a flea market on their honeymoon. They went up to Maine, I think. Bar Harbor or something like that. Maine is supposed to be gorgeous in the fall, with all the leaves changing colors. I've always loved real maple syrup, too. The actual stuff from the trees, not that high fructose corn syrup garbage they sell in the plastic bottles shaped like a lady. I don't even know why they still make that stuff... 

But, uh... what was I saying? Oh, right. No, I don't think doing something different would have stopped it. It just... it happened.

I look at him and I just think about how he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. It's not fair to him. \n\nHe's a good kid, too. He just started playing Little League this spring. Plays shortstop, just like I did when I was his age. I had this coach, Coach Miller—guy used to chew tobacco during the games and spit it right into a styrofoam cup in the dugout. It was disgusting. But he knew baseball. Taught me how to turn a double play. We actually made it to the state championships that year. We lost in the bottom of the ninth, though. Jimmy Peterson dropped a pop fly right out in right field. Kid was devastated. His mom brought orange slices for everyone after the game, but nobody even wanted to eat them. We just sat there on the grass, staring at the dirt. But yeah... it's just this constant heavy feeling. I hate that my nephew has to deal with me being like this.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like there was something you could have done to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I do. I mean, I've been an electrician for years. If all my years of experience couldn't keep my own brother safe, then what good is it? I came in here because I just can't shake the thought that I'm a danger to anybody who works with me now, or even anybody who lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of the roof, I really need to get up there and clean out the gutters. It had this stained glass shade with little blue dragonflies on it. My ex-wife absolutely hated it, said it was tacky, but I thought it gave off a really nice warm light in the hallway. It's just sitting in a box in the basement now, gathering dust. But... yeah. I just feel like I'm a hazard to everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you think you could have done something to prevent what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I do. I just... I keep going over it in my head, thinking about what I could have checked, what I should have noticed. It's like a loop. I found an old receipt of his stuffed behind one of the panels from a diner out on Route 9. The Starlight Diner, I think it was called. They used to make this incredible cherry pie. My dad would take me there sometimes on Sunday mornings when I was a kid, right after church. He always ordered his coffee black, said anything else ruined it. He had this old Chevy truck that smelled like motor oil and peppermint, because he always kept those little red and white mints in the glove compartment. I tried to find a truck like that a few years back, just for the nostalgia, you know? Looked all over Craigslist, even drove out to a farm in Ohio to check one out, but the rust was just eating right through the floorboards. The farmer had goats climbing all over it. Goats are weird animals, the way their eyes look. But... no. There wasn't anything I did or didn't do. It just... happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your brother's death would have been prevented if you had acted differently in some way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... that's exactly it. That's the whole reason I came in here to talk to you. I just can't shake the thought that if all my years as an electrician couldn't keep my own brother safe, then... then I'm a danger to anybody who works with me. Or even anyone who just lives under my roof. \n\nSpeaking of roofs, I actually had to get mine patched up last spring. We had this crazy hail storm that just came out of nowhere. Golf ball-sized hail, just completely destroying the shingles. The guy who came out to do the estimate, he drove this old El Camino. You really don't see those around much anymore, you know? It was painted this bright, cherry red. Reminded me of my cousin Tommy's car back in high school. Tommy was obsessed with restoring classic cars, but he was honestly terrible at it. He'd take the engine apart and then just leave all the parts scattered all over his mom's driveway for months. She'd get so mad because she couldn't park her minivan. She drove one of those early 90s Dodge Caravans with the fake wood paneling on the side. We took that thing on a road trip to Mount Rushmore once when we were kids. The AC broke halfway through South Dakota, and it was like ninety-five degrees out. We had to roll all the windows down, and the wind was just howling so loud you couldn't even hear the radio. My aunt kept trying to play these John Denver cassettes... \n\nBut, I mean, yeah. If my own experience wasn't enough to protect him, I just feel like I'm a walking hazard to everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific you did or didn't do during the incident with your brother that you think might have contributed to the outcome?\n\nPatient: No, there wasn't... I mean, I've racked my brain a million times, and I can't think of one single thing I actually did wrong. Or forgot to do. It's just... the whole thing. \n\nI was actually just looking at the tools I had with me that day. They're still sitting in the garage in this old metal toolbox my grandfather gave me. He was a machinist down in Philly for, like, forty years. Tough guy. He had this crazy scar on his forearm from where a piece of sheet metal caught him. He used to tell me he got it fighting a bear in the Poconos. I believed him until I was about ten. We used to go up to the Poconos every summer, actually, to this cabin near Lake Wallenpaupack. The mosquitoes up there were just massive. We used to burn those citronella candles, the ones in the little tin buckets, but they never really worked. My cousin got bit so bad once his eye swelled completely shut. He looked like he'd gone a few rounds with Mike Tyson. Tyson had that fight... when was it, '97? The one where he bit Holyfield's ear? Man, I remember watching that on pay-per-view at my buddy Dave's house. Dave made these incredible nachos with, like, three different kinds of cheese and jalapeños he grew in his own backyard. It's just this... this heavy mix of guilt and anger that just sits right here in my chest all day long. Especially when I look at my nephew. I look at him and I just think... he lost his dad, and now he's stuck with me instead. \n\nWe were actually trying to play catch in the yard the other day. Wonder if anyone will ever beat it... \n\nBut yeah, it's just... if I couldn't protect him, I feel like I can't protect anyone. He likes his toast practically burnt, which I don't get. Black as coal. Reminds me of this diner we used to go to when we were kids, off Route 9. The Silver Spoon. They had this cook, Sal, who always burnt the bacon. Swore it was the only way to eat it. Sal was a character. He had this anchor tattoo on his forearm that looked more like a squashed spider because the ink bled so bad over the years. He told us he got it in the Navy, stationed out in San Diego. San Diego is supposed to be beautiful. Perfect weather all year round. Seventy-two and sunny. I've always wanted to go out west, maybe see the Grand Canyon or something. Take one of those mule rides down to the bottom. Though I heard those mules can be pretty stubborn. A guy I used to work with, Dave, he went to the Grand Canyon and dropped his phone right over the edge trying to take a selfie. Brand new iPhone, too. He was so mad he didn't speak to his wife for the rest of the trip, like it was her fault he had slippery hands...\n\nBut yeah, anyway... it's just that same heavy feeling. Not about one thing I did wrong. Just... always there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel responsible for your brother's death?\n\nPatient: Honestly, doc, I don't know if \"responsible\" is the right word. I mean, I don't think it was my fault, like I didn't cause the accident. But I still just have that... that heavy feeling. Guilt, anger, whatever it is. It's just there. Especially with the kid. \n\nI took him to the park yesterday to just get out of the house. The one over by the lake, with the big wooden playground. They just put down all new mulch. Smelled like cedar. Reminded me of this cedar chest my grandma used to have at the foot of her bed. She kept all her old quilts in it. Man, she could quilt. She made this one for me when I graduated high school, had all these little squares made out of my old flannel shirts. I still have it in the hall closet. My dog, Buster, he used to love sleeping on that thing. Buster was a good boy. Golden retriever mix. Found him wandering around a construction site when I was an apprentice. He was just a puppy, chewing on a piece of drywall. Took him home, and he lived to be fourteen. Fourteen is a long time for a big dog. His hips went out at the end, though. Had to carry him up and down the porch stairs... \n\nBut yeah, I don't know if I feel responsible, exactly. I just... I just feel bad. All the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your brother's death wouldn't have happened if you weren't there?\n\nPatient: No, it's not that I think it wouldn't have happened if I wasn't there... it's more like... I came in here because I can't shake this thought. I've been an electrician for twenty years, right? I know what I'm doing. But if all those years of experience couldn't keep my own brother safe on a job site, then what good am I? I just keep thinking I'm a danger to anybody who works with me, or even lives under my roof. \n\nWhich is why I've been trying to fix up the house, make sure everything is perfect. I was up in the attic yesterday checking the wiring, just to be safe. It gets so hot up there. Reminded me of this summer I spent roofing with my uncle down in Florida. Man, the humidity down there is something else. You step outside and it's like breathing through a wet washcloth. My uncle, he loved it though. Said it was good for his joints. He had this old pontoon boat we used to take out on the weekends. We'd just sit out there for hours, trying to catch snapper. Never caught much, mostly just got sunburned. He had this cooler that he painted with the Miami Dolphins logo. Huge Dolphins fan. Had the Dan Marino jersey and everything. I think they went undefeated one year back in the seventies, right? The '72 Dolphins. Only team to ever do it. That's a crazy record to hold on to for that long. Wonder if anyone will ever beat it... \n\nBut yeah, it's just... if I couldn't protect him, I feel like I can't protect anyone. It's just a bad feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like his death wouldn't have happened if you had done something differently?\n\nPatient: I don't really think about it like that. It's not like I sit awake thinking, 'Oh, if I had just flipped that breaker' or 'if I used a different gauge wire.' I know the job was done by the book. I've had the same set of Klein tools for... man, it's gotta be fifteen years. Bought 'em at this little mom-and-pop hardware store on 4th Street. Old man Higgins ran the place. The whole store smelled like motor oil and sawdust, you know? The real deal. Not like these big box stores today where half the kids working there don't know a Phillips from a flathead. \n\nHiggins had this massive vintage Coca-Cola sign behind the counter. Said it was from the 1950s. The red paint was all chipped on the edges, but it was a classic. People used to offer him hundreds of bucks for it, but he wouldn't budge. He told me his wife bought it at a flea market on their honeymoon. They went up to Maine, I think. Bar Harbor or something like that. Maine is supposed to be gorgeous in the fall, with all the leaves changing colors. I've always loved real maple syrup, too. The actual stuff from the trees, not that high fructose corn syrup garbage they sell in the plastic bottles shaped like a lady. I don't even know why they still make that stuff... \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Oh, right. No, I don't think doing something different would have stopped it. It just... it happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like I said... I really don't think about it that way. I mean, I don't sit around trying to figure out what I could've done differently that day. The job was the job. We were working on that new commercial build out on Route 9, the one with the brick facade? They were using this reclaimed brick from an old factory downtown. Supposed to give it that rustic, industrial look everyone goes crazy for nowadays. 

My buddy Dave, he actually lives in one of those converted lofts downtown. Exposed pipes, concrete floors... the whole nine yards. He pays a fortune for it, too. I don't get the appeal, honestly. Walking on concrete all day is terrible for your knees. I had to buy these special gel inserts for my work boots just to get through the week. The brand is Timberland, I think. Good boots. Heavy, though. You wear those for ten hours and taking them off feels like taking off ankle weights. I used to wear ankle weights when I ran track in high school. Coach Miller swore by them. Said they'd make us faster. He was a character, Coach Miller. Always wore these bright yellow aviator sunglasses, even when it was cloudy... 

But, uh, no. I don't... I don't think me doing something different would've changed anything. It just... it is what it is.
